On the deformation theory of chiral quantizations
Dylan Butson and Sujay Nair

Abstract
. We give an operadic approach to deformation quantization of vertex Poisson algebras, a chiral analogue of the traditional problem of deformation quantization of Poisson algebras. Our main result is an order-by-order deformation-obstruction theory for such quantizations, controlled by the chiral analogue of Poisson cohomology. In the special case of chiral quantizations of affine symplectic varieties, quantizations of the vertex Poisson algebras of functions on their arc spaces, we prove that this deformation-obstruction theory is controlled by their de Rham cohomology. As another application, we prove that the boundary Virasoro minimal models are rigid under deformations.



1.
Introduction

Let
A
be a Poisson algebra, and recall that a
quantization
of
A
is an
\hbar
-adic associative algebra
A_{\hbar}
over the ring of formal power series
{\mathbb{K}}{[\![\hbar]\!]}
such that the central fibre
A_{0}=A_{\hbar}\otimes_{{\mathbb{K}}{[\![\hbar]\!]}}{\mathbb{K}}_{0}
is commutative, and thus canonically Poisson, with an isomorphism of as Poisson algebras
A_{0}\cong A
. Similarly, for
R
a vertex Poisson algebra, a
quantization
of
R
is an
\hbar
-adic vertex algebra
R_{\hbar}
such that the central fibre
R_{0}=R_{\hbar}\otimes_{{\mathbb{K}}{[\![\hbar]\!]}}{\mathbb{K}}_{0}
is a commutative vertex algebra, and thus canonically a vertex Poisson algebra, with an isomorphism of vertex Poisson algebras
R_{0}\cong R
.

In this work we view the quantization problem through the lens of deformation theory. In his ICM address
[
Tam03
]
, Tamarkin introduced the deformation-obstruction theory for chiral algebras, in the sense of Beilinson–Drinfeld
[
BD04
]
, a generalization of vertex algebras defined over smooth curves
X
which reduces to the traditional definition in the case
X=\mathbb{A}^{1}
. More recently, in
[
BDSHK19
]
, Bakalov–de Sole–Heluani–Kac developed this theory in detail in the case of vertex algebras.

In fact, Tamarkin conjectures an analogue of the celebrated Kontsevich formality theorem
[
Kon03
]
, and states that the importance of this conjecture can be seen from the corollary that deformations of chiral algebras are equivalent to those of coisson algebras, the analogous generalization of vertex Poisson algebras to other curves. The main theorem of this work achieves a somewhat similar goal, parameterizing the space of quantizations of a coisson algebra
R
, or in particular a vertex Poisson algebra, in terms of the cohomology
H^{\bullet}_{c}(R)
of its operadic deformation-obstruction complex:

Theorem 1.1
.

Let
R
be a vertex Poisson algebra and let
R_{\hbar/\hbar^{m}}
be a quantization of
R
defined modulo
\hbar^{m}
for
m\geq 1
. Then there exists a quantization of
R
defined modulo
\hbar^{m+1}
, agreeing with
R_{\hbar/\hbar^{m}}
modulo
\hbar^{m}
, if and only if a certain obstruction class determined by
R_{\hbar/\hbar^{m}}
vanishes, that is,

[{\textup{Obs}}(R_{\hbar/\hbar^{m}})]=0\ \in H^{2}_{c}(R)\penalty\ .

Further, if the obstruction class vanishes, the moduli space of such extensions, up to isomorphism, is a torsor for
H^{1}_{c}(R)
.

The space of functions
\mathcal{O}(\mathcal{J}Y)
on the arc space
\mathcal{J}Y
of an affine Poisson variety
Y
is naturally a vertex Poisson algebra, and this constitutes a rich source of examples of such. We call a quantization of the vertex Poisson algebra
\mathcal{O}(\mathcal{J}Y)
a
chiral quantization
of
Y
, following
[
Ara18
]
, though our definition is closer to that of strict chiral quantization in
loc. cit.
Chiral quantization has become increasingly relevant in the physics literature; see for example
[
BR18
]
.

In the case that
Y
is symplectic, we show that the order-by-order deformation-obstruction theory for chiral quantizations of
Y
is controlled by its de Rham cohomology:

Theorem 1.2
.

Let
Y
be an affine symplectic variety, and let
R_{\hbar/\hbar^{m}}
be a quantization of
\mathcal{O}(\mathcal{J}Y)
defined modulo
\hbar^{m}
for
m\geq 1
.
Then there exists a quantization of
\mathcal{O}(\mathcal{J}Y)
defined modulo
\hbar^{m+1}
, agreeing with
R_{\hbar/\hbar^{m}}
modulo
\hbar^{m}
, if and only if the obstruction class vanishes, that is,

[{\textup{Obs}}(R_{\hbar/\hbar^{m}})]=0\in H^{4}_{{\textup{dR}}}(Y)\penalty\ .

Further, if the obstruction class vanishes, the moduli space of such extensions, up to isomorphism, is a torsor for
H^{3}_{{\textup{dR}}}(Y)
.

This result is evocative of the classical work
[
dWL83
,
Fed94
,
Del95
,
BK04
]
on the deformation quantization of symplectic manifolds or varieties, which provide a full parametrization of the space of quantizations of
Y
in terms of
H^{2}_{\textup{dR}}(Y)
. It also provides a partial generalization of the results of
[
GMS00
,
GMS04
]
and
[
BD04
]
on vertex algebras of chiral differential operators, which implicitly classify the chiral quantizations of
Y
in the case
Y=T^{*}W
is a global cotangent bundle.

Although our result is abstractly weaker, in the sense that it fails to provide a trivialization of the iterated torsor describing formal quantizations, it is often still strong enough to draw the necessary conclusions about full spaces of quantizations in many applications.

Finally, we discuss the application of our results to boundary Virasoro minimal models: it has long been folklore that rational conformal field theories are isolated, and so in particular their vertex algebras are rigid under deformations. The boundary Virasoro minimal models are rational vertex algebras that are quantizations of a certain vertex Poisson algebra structure on the arc space of the thickened point
{\textup{Spec\ }}{\mathbb{K}}[T]/T^{n}
. Using the technical machinery developed in this paper, we prove the following result on the rigidity of these vertex algebras under deformations:

Theorem 1.3
.

Let
{\mathcal{M}}_{n}
be the boundary Virasoro minimal model for
(p,q)=(2,2n+1)
, the simple quotient of the Virasoro algebra
{\textup{Vir}}^{c}
at
c=c_{p,q}=1-\frac{6pq}{(p-q)^{2}}
. Then
{\mathcal{M}}_{n}
is rigid, that is, it does not admit non-trivial deformations.

1.1.
Relation to previous work

The problem of quantizing vertex Poisson algebras was first introduced by Li in
[
LI04
]
, following the analogy with deformation quantization of Poisson algebras.

In recent years, chiral quantization has become increasingly relevant in the physics literature. In the SCFT/VOA correspondence of
[
BLL+15
]
, the resulting vertex algebras are widely expected to be chiral quantizations of the Higgs branch of the four-dimensional theory. Similarly, in the boundary vertex algebra construction of
[
CG18
]
the resulting vertex algebras are chiral quantizations—albeit in a looser sense—of the Higgs and Coulomb branches.

Quantizations of vertex Poisson algebras also play an important role in the (micro)localization theory of vertex algebras; see for example
[
AKM15
,
AKM23
]
. Following
[
KR08
,
DK25
]
, the microlocalization of a vertex algebra is defined by considering it as a (sheafy) quantization of the sheaf of functions on the arc space of its associated scheme. Localization has been used to great effect, recently, to produce free-field realizations of a whole host of vertex algebras.

The deformation theory of chiral algebras was first introduced by Tamarkin in his ICM address
[
Tam03
]
, as mentioned above. In a series of works
[
BDSHK19
,
BDSHK20
,
BDSH+21
]
(subsets of) Bakalov, de Sole, Heluani, Kac, and Vignoli give a very concrete account of the deformation-obstruction complex for vertex algebras and vertex Poisson algebras.

A different cohomology theory for (graded) vertex algebras was developed by Huang in
[
Hua14a
,
Hua14b
]
, with the property that its second cohomology group parameterizes first order deformations, up to isomorphism. This cohomology theory has been studied by
[
KQ24
]
in the case of freely generated vertex algebras to parameterize their first order deformations. Recently,
[
LQ26
]
used Huang’s cohomology theory to show that simple affine vertex algebras with positive integral levels were rigid. Like our results on the minimal models, this gives further evidence, in the affirmative, for the belief that strongly rational vertex algebras are rigid.

As mentioned, our Theorem
1.2
is reminiscent of the classification of quantizations of symplectic varieties of
[
BK04
]
, following the approach of Fedosov
[
Fed94
]
for symplectic manifolds.
Optimistically, one could hope for an analogous construction for chiral quantizations, with an analogue of the noncommutative period map of
[
BK04
]
giving an explicit description of the moduli space of chiral quantizations. While this is not pursued in the current text, we believe that this could be a fruitful direction in future.

There have also been several approaches to the deformation theory of vertex algebras, and the quantization of vertex Poisson algebras, in terms of the BV formalism in mathematical physics. In
[
Li23
]
, Si Li proves that the quantum master equation for deformations of two-dimensional holomorphic field theories in the BV formalism is equivalent to the Maurer-Cartan equations for deformations of vertex algebras.

More recently, in
[
KZ25
]
, the authors define a three-dimensional classical field theory associated to a vertex Poisson algebra, in analogy with the construction of the two dimensional Poisson sigma model associated to a Poisson algebra. Both these classical field theories can also be defined more tersely in terms of the universal bulk theory construction of
[
BY17
]
. Optimistically, an analysis of the bulk-boundary Feynman diagrams in a quantization of this theory could give a quantization of the input vertex Poisson algebra structure, in analogy with the approach of Kontsevich
[
Kon03
]
.

Relatedly, and again optimistically, we hope that an analogue of the Kontsevich formality theorem could be proved in the chiral setting. In particular, we hope that an analogue of Tamarkin’s proof of Kontsevich formality
[
Tam98
,
Hin00
]
could also apply to the chiral setting.

1.2.
Outline

We begin in Section
2
with some recollections on operads, which form the backbone of many of our arguments. We also recall the classical examples of the operads governing familiar algebraic structures, such as associative, commutative, Lie, and Poisson algebras.

In Section
3
, we recall some preliminaries regarding D-modules on smooth algebraic varieties, with a particular focus on D-modules over an algebraic curve. In
3.2
we recall the various pseudo tensor structures on the category of D-modules on an algebraic curve
X
, and define chiral and coisson algebras.

In Section
4
, we recall the, perhaps more familiar, notions of vertex algebras and vertex Poisson algebras. Following
[
BD04
,
BDSHK19
]
, we also provide an operadic definition of these and relate them to the weakly translation invariant D-modules on
\mathbb{A}^{1}
. To conclude this section we present an initial definition of the quantization of a vertex Poisson algebra, following
[
AKM15
]
.

In Section
5
we introduce the DG Lie algebra
\mathfrak{g}_{{\mathcal{P}},{\mathcal{Q}}}
associated to a pair of
{\mathcal{P}}
and
{\mathcal{Q}}
, whose Maurer–Cartan elements parameterize the
{\mathcal{P}}
-algebra structures internal to
{\mathcal{Q}}
, and introduce the deformation-obstruction complex associated to a fixed
{\mathcal{P}}
-algebra internal to
{\mathcal{Q}}
.

We then review the deformation obstruction-complexes for familiar algebras and link them to well-known cohomology theories such as Hochschild and Chevalley–Eilenberg. We also relate the deformation-obstruction complex of a commutative algebra with its André–Quillen cohomology, and exposit some properties of the natural bicomplex underlying the deformation-obstruction complex of a Poisson algebra.

In Section
5.4
, we review the deformation-obstruction complexes for chiral and coisson algebras. As in the case of Poisson algebras, the deformation-obstruction complex for coisson algebras comes from a bicomplex, and shares many analogous properties.

In Section
6
, we review the role of the deformation-obstruction complex in controlling the higher order deformation-obstruction theory of algebras. In particular, we prove our main theorem on the order-by-order deformation theory of algebras, which we later adapt to the setting of quantizations.

In Section
7
, we prove Theorem
1.1
, which is Theorem
7.26
in the text. We first review the traditional case of deformation quantization of Poisson algebras and compare our order-by-order result, Theorem
7.6
, to that of Fedosov quantization.

We then proceed to the quantization of coisson algebras. We introduce a filtration on the chiral operad whose corresponding Rees object is used to describe quantizations of a vertex Poisson algebras. After some technical digressions on the splitting of this filtration, we provide the proof of Theorem
7.26
.

In Section
8
, we relate the variational cohomology of the arc space of a symplectic variety to its de Rham cohomology. This requires a digression on internal
\Hom
objects in the category of D-modules, as well as introducing internal versions of the classical operad and the variational cohomology.

We conclude in Section
9
with various the applications of our results. In particular, in Section
9.1
we prove Theorem
1.1
, which is Theorem
9.1
in the text, and in Section
9.5
, we prove Theorem
1.3
, which is Theorem
9.17
in the text.

Acknowledgements

The authors would like to thank Christopher Beem, whose advice during the early stages of this work was invaluable. We would also like to thank Tomoyuki Arakawa and Ivan Losev for useful discussions.

2.
Operadic preliminaries

Let
\mathcal{C}
be a symmetric monoidal category with monoidal structure
\otimes:\mathcal{C}\times\mathcal{C}\to\mathcal{C}
and unit
{\bf 1}_{\mathcal{C}}\in{\mathcal{C}}
. In applications, we will essentially always have
\mathcal{C}={\mathbb{K}}{\textup{-Mod}}
or
\mathcal{C}=(S{\textup{-Mod}},\otimes_{S})
for
S
a commutative
{\mathbb{K}}
-algebra, with
{\mathbb{K}}
a field of characteristic zero, or their differential graded (DG) variants.

2.1.
Plethystics

Let
\mathfrak{S}_{n}
denote the symmetric group on
n
letters. An
\mathfrak{S}
-module (internal to
{\mathcal{C}}
) also known as a collection,
M
, is a sequence
M=(M(0),M(1),\dots,M(n),\dots)
, where, for each
n\in\mathbb{N}
,
M(n)
is a representation of
\mathfrak{S}_{n}
internal to
{\mathcal{C}}
. The
\mathfrak{S}
-modules form a category,
\mathfrak{S}{\textup{-Mod}}
with morphisms,

\Hom_{{{\mathfrak{S}}}{\textup{-Mod}}}(M,N)=\bigoplus_{n}\Hom_{\mathcal{C}}(M(n),N(n))^{{{\mathfrak{S}}}_{n}}\penalty\ ,

for
M,N\in{{\mathfrak{S}}}{\textup{-Mod}}
. There are three notions of monoidal structure that one may be define on
{{\mathfrak{S}}}{\textup{-Mod}}
.

Given two
\mathfrak{S}
-modules
M,N
, their
tensor
product is the
\mathfrak{S}
-module

(M\underset{\mathfrak{S}}{\otimes}N)(n)\coloneq\bigoplus_{i+j=n}{\textup{Ind}}_{\mathfrak{S}_{i}\times\mathfrak{S}_{j}}^{\mathfrak{S}_{n}}M(i)\otimes N(j)

Then,
(\mathfrak{S}{\textup{-Mod}},\underset{\mathfrak{S}}{\otimes})
is symmetric monoidal with tensor unit
({\bf 1}_{\mathcal{C}},0\dots)
, the trivial
\mathfrak{S}
-module.

Given two
\mathfrak{S}
-modules
M,N
their
Hadamard
tensor product is the
\mathfrak{S}
-module

(M\otimes^{H}N)(n)\coloneqq M(n)\otimes N(n)\penalty\ ,

with
\mathfrak{S}_{n}
acting diagonally. Then,
(\mathfrak{S}{\textup{-Mod}},\otimes^{H})
is symmetric monoidal with tensor unit given by the collection
(\bf 1_{\mathcal{C}},1_{\mathcal{C}},\dots)
.

Given two
\mathfrak{S}
-modules
M,N
their
composite
product is the
\mathfrak{S}
-module

M\circ N\coloneq\bigoplus_{k\in\mathbb{N}}\bigg(M(k)\otimes N^{\underset{{{\mathfrak{S}}}}{\otimes}k}\bigg)_{\mathfrak{S}_{k}}\penalty\ ,

where
\mathfrak{S}_{k}
acts diagonally and acts on
N^{k}
by permuting factors. Let
\mathbbm{1}_{{\mathfrak{S}}}=(0,{\bf 1}_{\mathcal{C}},0,\dots)\in{{\mathfrak{S}}}{\textup{-Mod}}
, then for any
\mathfrak{S}
-module
M
,

\mathbbm{1}_{\mathfrak{S}}\circ M\cong M\circ\mathbbm{1}_{{\mathfrak{S}}}\cong M\penalty\ ,

and
(\mathfrak{S}{\textup{-Mod}},\circ,\mathbbm{1}_{\mathfrak{S}})
is a unital, monoidal (but not symmetric) category.

2.2.
Operads

Let
fSet
denote the category whose objects are finite sets with the obvious notion of morphisms between them. For a morphism
\pi:I\rightarrow J
, we write
I_{j}=\pi^{-1}(j)
for the fibre of
\pi
above
j\in J
.

Definition 2.1
.

A (symmetric, coloured)
operad

\mathcal{O}
in
\mathcal{C}
is:

(i)

A collection
{{\textup{col }}}\mathcal{O}
, elements of which are called
colours
or
objects
of
\mathcal{O}
,

(ii)

For each finite set
I\in{\textup{fSet}}
and indexed collection of objects
\{c_{i}\}_{i\in I}
and
d
of
\mathcal{O}
, an object
\mathcal{O}(\{c_{i}\}_{i\in I},d)\in\mathcal{C}
called the
multilinear operations
in
\mathcal{O}
.

(iii)

For each map of finite sets
I\to J
, and indexed collections of objects
\{c_{i}\}_{i\in I}
,
\{d_{j}\}_{j\in J}
and
e
, a morphism

\bigotimes_{j\in J}\mathcal{O}(\{c_{i}\}_{i\in I_{j}},d_{j})\otimes\mathcal{O}(\{d_{j}\}_{j\in J},e)\to\mathcal{O}(\{c_{I}\}_{i\in I},e)

of objects of
\mathcal{C}
called the
composition law
in
\mathcal{O}
.

(iv)

For each object
c\in\mathcal{C}
, a morphism
\mathbbm{1}_{c}\in\mathcal{O}(c,c)
called the
identity map
on
c
, which is both a left and right unit for the composition law.

(v)

For each sequence of maps
I\rightarrow J\rightarrow K
, and indexed collections of objects
\{c_{i}\}_{i\in I}
,
\{d_{j}\}_{j\in J}
,
\{e_{k}\}_{k\in K}
and
f
, compatible commutativity data for the diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\underset{j\in J}{\otimes}{\mathcal{O}}(\{c_{i}\}_{i\in J},d_{j})\otimes\underset{k\in K}{\otimes}{\mathcal{O}}(\{d_{j}\}_{j\in J_{k}},e_{k})\otimes{\mathcal{O}}(\{e_{k}\}_{k\in K},f)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\underset{k\in K}{\otimes}{\mathcal{O}}(\{c_{i}\}_{i\in I_{k}},e_{k})\otimes{\mathcal{O}}(\{e_{k}\}_{k\in K},f)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\underset{j\in J}{\otimes}{\mathcal{O}}(\{c_{i}\}_{i\in I_{j}},d_{j})\otimes{\mathcal{O}}(\{d_{j}\},f)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{O}}(\{c_{i}\}_{i\in I},f)}

and similarly for higher arity compositions.

A map
\varphi:\mathcal{O}\to\mathcal{O}^{\prime}
of operads in
\mathcal{C}
is:

(i)

A map
{{\textup{col }}}\mathcal{O}\to{{\textup{col }}}\mathcal{O}^{\prime}

(ii)

For each map of finite sets
I\to J
, and indexed collections of objects
\{c_{i}\}_{i\in I}
and
d
of
\mathcal{O}
, a morphism

\mathcal{O}(\{c_{i}\}_{i\in I_{j}},d)\to\mathcal{O}^{\prime}(\{\varphi(c_{i})\}_{i\in I_{j}},\varphi(d))

such that
\mathbbm{1}_{c}
maps to
\mathbbm{1}_{\varphi(c)}
for each
c\in{{\textup{col }}}\mathcal{O}
.

(iii)

For each map of finite sets
I\to J
, and indexed collections of objects
\{c_{i}\}_{i\in I}
,
\{d_{j}\}_{j\in J}
and
e
, the commutativity of the diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\underset{j\in J}{\otimes}\mathcal{O}(\{c_{i}\}_{i\in I_{j}},d_{j})\otimes\mathcal{O}(\{d_{j}\}_{j\in J},e)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{O}(\{c_{i}\}_{i\in I},e)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\underset{j\in J}{\otimes}\mathcal{O}^{\prime}(\{\varphi(c_{i})\}_{i\in I_{j}},\varphi(d_{j}))\otimes\mathcal{O}(\{\varphi(d_{j})\}_{j\in J},\varphi(e))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{O}^{\prime}(\{\varphi(c_{i})\}_{i\in I},\varphi(e))}

The collection of operads in
\mathcal{C}
thus defines a category, denoted
\mathrm{Op}(\mathcal{C})
. Let
{\textup{Alg}}_{\mathcal{O}}(\mathcal{O}^{\prime})
denote the space
\Hom_{\mathrm{Op}(\mathcal{C})}(\mathcal{O},\mathcal{O}^{\prime})
of maps of operads
\mathcal{O}\to\mathcal{O}^{\prime}
.

For an operad
\mathcal{O}
with a single colour
{{\textup{col }}}\mathcal{O}=\{c\}
, we use the notation

\mathcal{O}(n)=\mathcal{O}(\{c\}_{i\in\{1,\dots,n\}},c)\penalty\ .

Note that
\mathcal{O}(n)
is an
{{\mathfrak{S}}}_{n}
-module internal to
\mathcal{C}
, and so the sequence,
{\mathcal{O}}=({\mathcal{O}}(1),{\mathcal{O}}(2),\dots)
, is an
{{\mathfrak{S}}}
-module.

Remark 2.2
.

Indeed, operads on a single colour admit a slicker definition: a single coloured operad is equivalent to a unital, monoidal object in the monoidal category
({{\mathfrak{S}}}{\textup{-Mod}},\circ)
. Namely, a single coloured operad is a tuple
({\mathcal{O}},\mu,u)
, where
{\mathcal{O}}\in{{\mathfrak{S}}}{\textup{-Mod}}
and
\mu:{\mathcal{O}}\circ{\mathcal{O}}\rightarrow{\mathcal{O}}
is an associative product with unit
u:\mathbbm{1}_{{\mathfrak{S}}}\rightarrow{\mathcal{O}}
.

A natural source of coloured operads are enriched symmetric monoidal categories.

Example 2.3
.

Let
{\mathcal{D}}
be a symmetric monoidal category enriched over
\mathcal{C}
. Then
{\mathcal{D}}
defines an operad
\mathcal{O}_{{\mathcal{D}}}\in\mathrm{Op}(\mathcal{C})
in
\mathcal{C}
, with objects given by objects of
{\mathcal{D}}
and multilinear operations given by

\mathcal{O}_{{\mathcal{D}}}(\{C_{i}\}_{i\in I},D)=\Hom_{{\mathcal{D}}}(\otimes_{i\in I}C_{i},D)\ .

In this case, we abbreviate
{\textup{Alg}}_{\mathcal{O}}(\mathcal{O}_{{\mathcal{D}}})={\textup{Alg}}_{\mathcal{O}}({\mathcal{D}})
. In particular, for any object
D\in\mathcal{D}
we let
{\textup{End}}_{\mathcal{D}}(D)
denote the full suboperad on the single colour
D
, with space of
n
-ary operations given by

{\textup{End}}_{\mathcal{D}}(D)(n)=\Hom_{\mathcal{D}}(D^{\otimes n},D)\ .

Example 2.4
.

Following Example
2.3
above, if
\mathcal{C}
is a closed monoidal category, then it defines an operad in itself and thus for any operad
\mathcal{O}
in
\mathcal{C}
we have a canonical category
{\textup{Alg}}_{\mathcal{O}}(\mathcal{C}):={\textup{Alg}}_{\mathcal{O}}(\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{C}})
of algebras over
\mathcal{O}
in
\mathcal{C}
.

Furthermore, if
\mathcal{O}
is an operad internal to
\mathcal{C}
on a single colour, then an object
A\in{\textup{Alg}}_{\mathcal{O}}(\mathcal{C})
is equivalent to an object
A\in\mathcal{C}
together with maps in
\mathcal{C}

(2.1)

\mathcal{O}(n)\to{\mathcal{H}\textup{om}}_{\mathcal{C}}(A^{\otimes n},A)

such that the composition of operations in
\mathcal{O}
is mapped to the internal composition of multi-linear maps in the closed symmetric monoidal category
\mathcal{C}
.

Remark 2.5
.

We think of the multilinear maps from
A
to itself induced by the image of the morphism in Equation
2.1
as equipping the object
A
with an algebraic structure—hence the notation.
These multilinear maps specify all possible
n
-ary operations on
A
that are built up from some set of elementary operations, modulo any relations imposed on them.

2.2.1.
Generators and relations

Since every single coloured operad has an underlying
{{\mathfrak{S}}}
-module, we have a forgetful functor

\mathrm{Op}({\mathcal{C}})^{\textup{s.\ col}}\rightarrow{{\mathfrak{S}}}{\textup{-Mod}}\penalty\ .

The left adjoint to this functor is the free-operad construction:

Definition 2.6
.

The free operad on
E\in{{\mathfrak{S}}}{\textup{-Mod}}
,
\mathcal{{\mathcal{F}}}(E)\in\mathrm{Op}({\mathcal{C}})
, is a single coloured operad characterized by the property that for any single coloured operad,
{\mathcal{O}}\in\mathrm{Op}({\mathcal{C}})
,

{\textup{Alg}}_{\mathcal{F}(E)}(\mathcal{O})=\Hom_{{{\mathfrak{S}}}{\textup{-Mod}}}(E,{\mathcal{O}})\penalty\ .

The free operad admits an inductive construction, with
{\mathcal{F}}(E)={\textup{colim}}_{m\in\mathbb{N}}{\mathcal{F}}_{m}(E)
, where

{\mathcal{F}}_{0}(E)=\mathbbm{1}_{{\mathfrak{S}}}\penalty\ ,\text{ and }{\mathcal{F}}_{m}(E)=\mathbbm{1}_{{\mathfrak{S}}}\oplus(E\circ{\mathcal{F}}_{m-1}(E))\text{ for }m>0\penalty\ .

We say a single coloured operad
\mathcal{O}
is generated over a collection,
E
, if there exists some
{{\mathfrak{S}}}
-submodule
R\hookrightarrow{\mathcal{F}}(E)
, generating an operadic ideal
(R)\subset{\mathcal{F}}(E)
, such that

{\mathcal{O}}={\mathcal{F}}(E)/(R)\penalty\ .

In particular, an operad is
binary
if it is generated by a collection of the form,
E=(0,0,E(2),0\dots)
,
i.e.
, it is generated by binary operations.

2.2.2.
Pseudo-tensor categories

Let
{\mathcal{D}}
be a symmetric monoidal category enriched over
{\mathcal{C}}
. From Example
2.3
we know that
{\mathcal{D}}
defines an operad,
{\mathcal{O}}_{\mathcal{D}}
in
{\mathcal{C}}
. Now let
{\mathcal{D}}^{\prime}\subset{\mathcal{D}}
be a subcategory that is not necessarily closed under the monoidal structure of
{\mathcal{D}}
. While
{\mathcal{D}}^{\prime}
is not a monoidal subcategory, it still defines a suboperad
{\mathcal{O}}_{{\mathcal{D}}^{\prime}}{\ \hookrightarrow\ }{\mathcal{O}}_{\mathcal{D}}
with spaces of operations

\mathcal{O}_{{\mathcal{D}}^{\prime}}(\{c_{i}\}_{i\in I},d)=\Hom_{{\mathcal{D}}}(\otimes_{i\in I}c_{i},d)\ .

Again, we abbreviate
{\textup{Alg}}_{\mathcal{O}}(\mathcal{O}_{{\mathcal{D}}^{\prime}})={\textup{Alg}}_{\mathcal{O}}({\mathcal{D}}^{\prime})
.

One should therefore think of a general (coloured, symmetric) operad as a natural generalization of a symmetric monoidal category,
i.e.
, the correct gadget internal to which we can define various kinds of algebras. For this reason coloured symmetric operads are also called pseudo-tensor categories, for example in
[
BD04
]
.

Our primary objects of consideration will be algebraic structures of the form
A\in{\textup{Alg}}_{{\mathcal{P}}}({\mathcal{Q}})
where
{\mathcal{P}}
is an operad with a single colour encoding a familiar algebraic structure (such as Lie or Poisson algebras), and
{\mathcal{Q}}
is a coloured operad enhancing some familiar category of interest, such as
\Vect_{\mathbb{K}}
or D-modules on an algebraic curve. Thus, we make the following formal definition:

Definition 2.7
.

Let
{\mathcal{D}}
be a category enriched over
{\mathcal{C}}
. A pseudo-tensor structure on
{\mathcal{D}}
is a coloured operad
\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{D}}\in\mathrm{Op}({\mathcal{C}})
such that

(i)

the collection of colours
{{\textup{col }}}\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{D}}
is equal to the collection of objects of
\mathcal{D}
,

(ii)

the spaces of 1-ary operations
\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{D}}(M,N)
and their compositions are given by the spaces of morphisms in
{\mathcal{D}}
and their categorical composition, respectively.

In particular, given any object
M\in\mathcal{D}
, we let
{\textup{End}}_{\mathcal{D}}(M)
denote the full suboperad on the single colour
M
, whose space of
n
-ary operations is given by

(2.2)

{\textup{End}}_{\mathcal{D}}(M)(n)=\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{D}}(\{M\}_{i=1}^{n},M)\ .

Evidently, every symmetric monoidal category defines a pseudo-tensor category by Example
2.3
.

2.3.
Algebras of the classical kind

We introduce some examples of binary operads and their respective algebras. These operads will be introduced over
{\mathcal{C}}=\Vect_{{\mathbb{K}}}
with its usual tensor product and unit. Throughout, we let
{\mathcal{D}}
denote a
{\mathbb{K}}
-linear symmetric monoidal category.

2.3.1.

The commutative operad
{\textup{Comm}}\in\mathrm{Op}(\Vect_{\mathbb{K}})
is generated by

E=(0,0,{\mathbb{K}}_{m},0,\dots)\penalty\ ,

where
{\mathbb{K}}_{m}
is the trivial representation of
{{\mathfrak{S}}}_{2}
, subject to the relation generated by

{\mathbb{K}}[{{\mathfrak{S}}}_{3}]\cdot{\textup{Ass}}(m)\subset{\mathcal{F}}(E)(3)\qquad\text{ defined by }\qquad{\textup{Ass}}(m)=m\circ(m\otimes\mathbbm{1})-m\circ(\mathbbm{1}\otimes m)\in{\mathcal{F}}(E)(3)\penalty\ .

The category of
Comm
algebras
{\textup{Comm}}(\mathcal{D}):={\textup{Alg}}_{{\textup{Comm}}}(\mathcal{D})
in
\mathcal{D}
is the usual category of (non-unital) commutative algebra objects in
\mathcal{D}
.

As
{{\mathfrak{S}}}
-modules

{\textup{Comm}}=({\mathbb{K}})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\penalty\ ,

where
{\mathbb{K}}
is the trivial representation of each
{{\mathfrak{S}}}_{n}
.

2.3.2.

The Lie operad
{\textup{Lie}}\in\mathrm{Op}(\Vect_{\mathbb{K}})
is generated by

E=(0,0,{\mathbb{K}}_{b},0,\dots)\penalty\ ,

where
{\mathbb{K}}_{b}
is the sign representation of
{{\mathfrak{S}}}_{2}
, subject to the relation generated by

{\mathbb{K}}[{{\mathfrak{S}}}_{3}]\cdot\text{Jac}(b)\subset\mathcal{F}(E)(3)\qquad\text{ defined by }\qquad\text{Jac}(b)=b\circ(\mathbbm{1}\otimes b)-b\circ(b\otimes\mathbbm{1})-b\circ(\mathbbm{1}\otimes b)\circ\sigma_{12}\in{\mathcal{F}}(E)(3)\penalty\ .

The category
{\textup{Lie}}({\mathcal{D}})\coloneqq{\textup{Alg}}_{\textup{Lie}}({\mathcal{D}})
, is the usual category of Lie algebra objects in
{\mathcal{D}}
.

2.3.3.

The associative operad
{\textup{Ass}}\in\mathrm{Op}(\Vect_{\mathbb{K}})
is generated by

E=(0,0,{\mathbb{K}}[{{\mathfrak{S}}}_{2}]\cdot m,0,\dots)\penalty\ ,

subject to the relation generated by

{\mathbb{K}}[{{\mathfrak{S}}}_{3}]\cdot{\textup{Ass}}(m)\subset{\mathcal{F}}(E)(3)\quad\quad\text{ defined by }\quad\quad\text{Ass}(m)=m\circ(m\otimes\mathbbm{1})-m\circ(\mathbbm{1}\otimes m)\in\mathcal{F}(E)(3)\penalty\ .

The category
{\textup{Ass}}(\mathcal{D}):={\textup{Alg}}_{{\textup{Ass}}}(\mathcal{D})
in
\mathcal{D}
is the usual category of associative algebra objects in
\mathcal{D}
.

As
{{\mathfrak{S}}}
-modules,

{\textup{Ass}}=\big({\mathbb{K}}[{{\mathfrak{S}}}_{n}]\big)_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\penalty\ ,

i.e.
, the regular representation of
{{\mathfrak{S}}}_{n}
in arity
n
.

We have a sequence of operads over
{\mathbb{K}}
,

{\textup{Lie}}\hookrightarrow{\textup{Ass}}\twoheadrightarrow{\textup{Comm}}\penalty\ .

The surjection
{\textup{Ass}}\twoheadrightarrow{\textup{Comm}}
induces a fully faithful embedding

{\textup{Comm}}({\mathcal{D}})\hookrightarrow{\textup{Ass}}({\mathcal{D}})\penalty\ ,

by forgetting the commutativity property.

Meanwhile, the inclusion
{\textup{Lie}}\hookrightarrow{\textup{Ass}}
induces a forgetful functor

{\textup{Ass}}({\mathcal{D}})\rightarrow{\textup{Lie}}({\mathcal{D}})\penalty\ ,

sending an associative algebra
A
to the Lie algebra
(A,[\cdot,\cdot])
, where
[\cdot,\cdot]
is the commutator of the associative product on
A
. This forgetful functor admits a left adjoint given by the universal enveloping algebra construction

\displaystyle{\textup{Lie}}({\mathcal{D}})

\displaystyle\rightarrow{\textup{Ass}}({\mathcal{D}})\penalty\ ,

\displaystyle\mathfrak{h}

\displaystyle\mapsto U(\mathfrak{h})\penalty\

2.3.4.

The Poisson operad
{\textup{Pois}}\in\mathrm{Op}(\Vect_{\mathbb{K}})
, is generated by

E=(0,0,{\mathbb{K}}_{m}\oplus{\mathbb{K}}_{\pi},0\dots)

where
{\mathbb{K}}_{m}
is the trivial representation and
{\mathbb{K}}_{\pi}
is the sign representation, subject to the relations generated by

{\mathbb{K}}[{{\mathfrak{S}}}_{3}]\cdot\big(\text{Jac}(\pi)\oplus{\textup{Ass}}(m)\oplus\text{Dist}(m,\pi)\big)\subset{\mathcal{F}}(E)(3)\penalty\ ,

defined by

\displaystyle\text{Jac}(\pi)

\displaystyle=\pi\circ(\mathbbm{1}\otimes\pi)-\pi\circ(\pi\otimes\mathbbm{1})-\pi\circ(\mathbbm{1}\otimes\pi)\circ\sigma_{12}\ ,

\displaystyle\text{Ass}(m)

\displaystyle=m\circ(m\otimes\mathbbm{1})-m\circ(\mathbbm{1}\otimes m)\penalty\ ,

\displaystyle\text{Dist}(m,\pi)

\displaystyle=\pi\circ(\mathbbm{1}\otimes m)-m\circ(\pi\otimes\mathbbm{1})-m\circ(\mathbbm{1}\otimes\pi)\ .

The Poisson operad admits an auxiliary grading

{\textup{Pois}}=\bigoplus_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\bigoplus_{p=0}^{n}{\textup{Pois}}(n)^{\langle p\rangle}\penalty\ ,

defined by assigning the grading
E=E^{\langle 0\rangle}\oplus E^{\langle-1\rangle}
to the generators, with

E^{\langle 0\rangle}=(0,0,{\mathbb{K}}_{m},0,\dots)\penalty\ ,\qquad\text{and}\qquad E^{\langle-1\rangle}=(0,0,{\mathbb{K}}_{\pi},0\dots)\penalty\ .

The category
{\textup{Pois}}({\mathcal{D}})\coloneqq{\textup{Alg}}_{{\textup{Pois}}}({\mathcal{D}})
is the usual category of Poisson algebra objects in
{\mathcal{D}}
. In particular, the category
{\textup{Alg}}_{{\textup{Pois}}}(\Vect_{\mathbb{K}})
is evidently given by the usual category of Poisson algebras, that is, commutative algebras
A\in{\textup{Alg}}_{\textup{Comm}}(\Vect_{\mathbb{K}})
together with a Lie bracket
\pi:A^{\otimes 2}\to A
such that
\pi
is a derivation of the product
m
.

Similarly, the category
{\textup{Alg}}^{\textup{gr}}_{{\textup{Pois}}}(\Vect_{\mathbb{K}}^{\textup{gr}})
of graded Poisson algebras is the usual category of graded commutative algebras equipped with a Lie bracket
\pi:A^{\otimes 2}\to A
of graded degree
-1
which is a derivation of the commutative product. For later, we note that this is the natural graded degree of the Poisson bracket when the Poisson algebra is the associative graded of a filtered associative algebra.

The Poisson operad fits into the following sequence of operads over
{\mathbb{K}}
,

\displaystyle{\textup{Lie}}\hookrightarrow{\textup{Pois}}\twoheadrightarrow{\textup{Comm}}

The surjection induces the fully faithful embedding

{\textup{Comm}}({\mathcal{D}})\hookrightarrow{\textup{Pois}}({\mathcal{D}})\penalty\ ,

by regarding a commutative algebra as a Poisson algebra with trivial bracket.

The inclusion induces a forgetful functor

{\textup{Pois}}({\mathcal{D}})\rightarrow{\textup{Lie}}({\mathcal{D}})\penalty\ ,

by forgetting the commutative multiplication. As with associative algebras, this functor admits a left adjoint

\displaystyle{\textup{Lie}}({\mathcal{D}})\rightarrow{\textup{Pois}}({\mathcal{D}})\penalty\ ,\mathfrak{h}\mapsto\textup{Sym}(\mathfrak{h})

and equipping
\textup{Sym}(\mathfrak{h})
with the Kirillov–Kostant–Souriau Poisson bracket.

3.
Algebras of the chiral kind

3.1.
The category of D-modules

Let
X
be a smooth variety of dimension
d_{X}
over
\mathbb{K}={\mathbb{C}}
or a field of characteristic
0
. We write
\mathcal{O}_{X}
for the sheaf of regular functions,
\mathcal{D}_{X}
for the sheaf of differential operators,
\Theta_{X}
for the tangent sheaf,
\Omega^{1}_{X}
for the sheaf of Kähler differentials,
\Omega^{d_{X}}_{X}
for the sheaf of sections of the canonical bundle, and
\omega_{X}=\Omega^{d_{X}}_{X}[d_{X}]
for the dualizing sheaf on
X
. Let
{\textup{D}}(\mathcal{O}_{X})
be the derived category of complexes of
\mathcal{O}_{X}
-modules, and
{\textup{Sh}}_{\textup{z}}(X)
the category of complexes of sheaves of
\mathbb{K}
-modules on
X
in the Zariski topology. We will typically take
X
to be a smooth algebraic curve, though we will also need to consider D-modules on more general varieties and thus we will recall the basic conventions to fix notation in general.

Definition 3.1
.

Let
f:X\to Y
a map of schemes. The inverse and direct image functors are

\mathbb{L}f^{\bullet}:{\textup{D}}(\mathcal{O}_{Y})\to{\textup{D}}(\mathcal{O}_{X})\qquad f^{\bullet}\mathcal{F}=f^{-1}F\otimes_{f^{-1}\mathcal{O}_{Y}}\mathcal{O}_{X}\qquad\text{and}\qquad\mathbb{R}f_{\bullet}:{\textup{D}}(\mathcal{O}_{X})\to{\textup{D}}(\mathcal{O}_{Y})\qquad f_{\bullet}\mathcal{F}=f_{\bullet}\mathcal{F}\ ,

where
f_{\bullet}:{\textup{Sh}}_{\textup{z}}(X)\to{\textup{Sh}}_{\textup{z}}(Y)
and
f^{-1}:{\textup{Sh}}_{\textup{z}}(Y)\to{\textup{Sh}}_{\textup{z}}(X)
are the usual direct and inverse image functors on sheaves of
\mathbb{K}
-modules.

Let
{D}^{\ell}(X)
(respectively,
{\textup{D}}^{\ell}(X)
) denote the (derived) category of (complexes of) left D-modules, that is, (complexes of) sheaves of left modules on
X
over the sheaf of algebras
\mathcal{D}_{X}
, such that the underlying (cohomology) sheaves of
\mathcal{O}_{X}
-modules are quasi-coherent. Similarly, we let
{D}^{r}(X)
(respectively,
{\textup{D}}^{r}(X)
) denote the (derived) category of (complexes of) right D-modules.

There is an equivalence of categories
{\textup{D}}^{\ell}(X)\xrightarrow{\cong}{\textup{D}}^{r}(X)
sending a complex of left D-modules
M^{\ell}\in{\textup{D}}^{\ell}(X)
to the complex
M^{r}=M^{\ell}\otimes_{{\mathcal{O}}_{X}}\omega_{X}\in{\textup{D}}^{r}(X)
, and inducing an identification of the respective hearts up to a shift
{D}^{\ell}(X)[-d_{X}]\xrightarrow{\cong}{D}^{r}(X)
. We write
{\textup{D}}(X)
for the common value of the derived category under this identification, though we will reference the specific models when defining functors between these categories.

We also use an alternative equivalence identifying the left and right hearts
{D}^{\ell}(X)\xrightarrow{\cong}{D}^{r}(X)
, sending a left
D
-module
M^{\ell}\in{D}^{\ell}(X)
to
M^{r,\heartsuit}=M^{\ell}\otimes_{\mathcal{O}_{X}}\Omega^{d_{X}}_{X}=M^{r}[-d_{X}]\in{D}^{r}(X)
. We write
{D}(X)={D}^{r}(X)
for the abelian category given by the right heart, though we will also use the inverse identification

(\cdot)^{\ell,\heartsuit}:{D}(X)\xrightarrow{\cong}{D}^{\ell}(X)\qquad\text{where}\qquad M^{\ell,\heartsuit}=M^{\ell}[d_{X}]

to define various structures on
{D}(X)
.
One should view this identification as less canonical than the derived analogue, but it will nonetheless be necessary to establish the theory of plain (non-DG) algebras of the chiral kind.

Definition 3.2
.

Let
f:X\to Y
be a map of smooth varieties. The inverse image
f^{!}:{\textup{D}}(Y)\to{\textup{D}}(X)
is defined by

f^{!}=\mathbb{L}f^{!,\heartsuit}:{\textup{D}}^{\ell}(Y)\to{\textup{D}}^{\ell}(X)\qquad\text{where}\qquad f^{!,\heartsuit}(M)=f^{\bullet}(M)\quad\text{equipped with the pullback flat connection.}

Evidently this functor is right exact with respect to the left t-structure, and we recall that for
f:X\to Y
smooth it is exact.

Definition 3.3
.

The exterior product is defined by

\boxtimes:{\textup{D}}(X)\times{\textup{D}}(Y)\to{\textup{D}}(X\times Y)\qquad\text{where}\qquad M\boxtimes N=\pi_{X}^{!}M\otimes\pi_{Y}^{!}N\ ,

for
\pi_{X}:X\times Y\to X,\pi_{Y}:X\times Y\to Y
. Note that for
X
and
Y
smooth, the maps
\pi_{X}
and
\pi_{Y}
are smooth and thus the relevant pullback functors are exact, and by abuse of notation we also define

\boxtimes:{D}(X)\times{D}(Y)\to{D}(X\times Y)\qquad\text{where}\qquad M\boxtimes N=\pi_{X}^{!}M\otimes\pi_{Y}^{!}N\ .

The derived category
{\textup{D}}(X)
is symmetric monoidal with respect to the derived tensor product

M\otimes^{!,\mathbb{L}}N=\Delta^{!}(M\boxtimes N)

for
M,N\in{\textup{D}}(X)
and
\Delta:X\to X\times X
the diagonal embedding.

The abelian category
{D}(X)
is also symmetric monoidal with respect to the non-derived tensor product

M\otimes^{!}N=\Delta^{!,\heartsuit}(M^{\ell,\heartsuit}\boxtimes N^{\ell,\heartsuit})\ .

Concretely, for
L,M\in{D}^{\ell}(X)
, we have
L\otimes^{!}M=L\underset{{\mathcal{O}}_{X}}{\otimes}M

as
{\mathcal{O}}_{X}
-modules and with
\tau\in\Theta_{X}\subset{\mathcal{D}}_{X}
acting via the usual Leibniz rule,
\tau\otimes 1+1\otimes\tau
.

Note that for
M\in{D}(X)
locally projective we have
M\otimes^{!}N\cong M\otimes^{\mathbb{L},!}N[d_{X}]
.

Definition 3.4
.

Let
f:X\to Y
be a map of smooth varieties. The direct image
f_{*}:{\textup{D}}(X)\to{\textup{D}}(Y)
is defined by

f_{*}:{\textup{D}}^{r}(X)\to{\textup{D}}^{r}(Y)\quad\quad f_{*}(M)=\mathbb{R}f_{\bullet}(M\otimes^{\mathbb{L}}_{\mathcal{D}_{X}}\mathcal{D}_{X\to Y})\qquad\text{for}\qquad\mathcal{D}_{X\to Y}:=f^{!,\heartsuit}\mathcal{D}_{Y}\in(\mathcal{D}_{X},f^{-1}\mathcal{D}_{Y})\text{-Mod}\,

where
\mathcal{D}_{X\to Y}=f^{!,\heartsuit}\mathcal{D}_{Y}\in{D}(X)
is defined in terms of
\mathcal{D}_{Y}\in{D}^{\ell}(Y)
as a left module, so that the
(\mathcal{D}_{Y},\mathcal{D}_{Y})
-bimodule structure on
\mathcal{D}_{Y}
equips
\mathcal{D}_{X\to Y}
with the structure of a
(\mathcal{D}_{X},f^{-1}\mathcal{D}_{Y})
-bimodule.

Recall that for
f
quasi-finite the functor
f_{*}
is left exact, and for
f
affine it is right exact, with respect to the right t-structure. Further, recall that for a closed embedding the functor
f_{*}
is exact with respect to the right t-structure, and defines an equivalence
f_{*}:{D}(X)\xrightarrow{\cong}{D}(Y)_{X}
of abelian categories between
{D}(X)
and the full subcategory
{D}(Y)_{X}\subset{D}(Y)
of right D-modules on
Y
supported on
X
, with inverse equivalence
f^{!}_{X}=f^{!}|_{{D}(Y)_{X}}:{D}(Y)_{X}\xrightarrow{\cong}{D}(X)
.

Definition 3.5
.

For
\pi:X\to{\textup{pt}}
and
M\in{\textup{D}}(X)
we define the de Rham cochains of
M
by

C^{\bullet}_{\textup{dR}}(X,M)=\pi_{*}M\in{\textup{D}}({\textup{pt}})={\textup{D}}({\mathbb{K}}{\textup{-Mod}})\ .

Concretely, for
M\in{\textup{D}}^{r}(X)
we have

\pi_{*}M=\mathbb{R}\pi_{\bullet}(M^{r}\otimes^{\mathbb{L}}_{\mathcal{D}_{X}}\mathcal{O}_{X})\cong\mathbb{R}\pi_{\bullet}(M^{\ell}\otimes_{\mathcal{O}_{X}}\Omega^{\bullet}_{X})[2d_{X}]

where
M^{\ell}\otimes_{\mathcal{O}_{X}}\Omega^{\bullet}_{X}\in{\textup{D}}({\textup{Sh}}_{\textup{z}}(X))
denotes the algebraic de Rham complex with coefficients in the complex
M^{\ell}
, considered as a complex of Zariski sheaves on
X
, and the latter isomorphism follows from using the Spencer–Koszul resolution,

\mathcal{K}^{\bullet}=\left[\mathcal{D}_{X}\otimes_{\mathcal{O}_{X}}\wedge^{d_{X}}_{\mathcal{O}_{X}}\theta_{X}[d_{X}]\to\cdots\to\mathcal{D}_{X}\otimes_{\mathcal{O}_{X}}\wedge^{2}_{\mathcal{O}_{X}}\theta_{X}[2]\to\mathcal{D}_{X}\otimes_{\mathcal{O}_{X}}\theta_{X}[1]\to\mathcal{D}_{X}\right]\xrightarrow{\cong}\mathcal{O}_{X}\ ,

to compute the derived tensor product as

M\otimes^{\mathbb{L}}_{\mathcal{D}_{X}}\mathcal{O}_{X}\cong M\otimes_{\mathcal{D}_{X}}\mathcal{K}^{\bullet}\cong M^{\ell}\otimes_{\mathcal{O}_{X}}\omega_{X}\otimes_{\mathcal{D}_{X}}\mathcal{K}^{\bullet}\cong M^{\ell}\otimes_{\mathcal{O}_{X}}\Omega^{\bullet}_{X}[2d_{X}]\ .

We also define the middle de Rham cohomology as the functor of abelian categories

h:{D}(X)\to{D}({\textup{pt}})={\mathbb{K}}{\textup{-Mod}}\qquad\text{by}\qquad h(M)=\pi_{\bullet}(M^{r}\otimes_{\mathcal{D}_{X}}\mathcal{O}_{X})\cong\pi_{\bullet}(M^{r}/(M^{r}\cdot\theta_{X}))

for
M=M^{r}\in{D}(X)\cong{D}^{r}(X)
, noting
\mathcal{O}_{X}\cong\mathcal{D}_{X}/(\mathcal{D}_{X}\cdot\theta_{X})
. This functor computes the true de Rham cohomology in the following setting:

Proposition 3.6
.

Let
X
be a smooth, affine variety and
M\in{D}(X)
be locally projective. Then there is a natural quasi-isomorphism

\pi_{*}M\xrightarrow{\cong}h(M)\ .

3.2.
Chiral,
{\textup{Comm}}^{!}
, and
{\textup{Lie}}^{*}
algebras

3.2.1.
\text{Comm}^{!}
algebras

Let
{D}(X)^{!}
denote the pseudo-tensor structure on
{D}(X)
induced by the symmetric monoidal structure
\otimes^{!}
. Concretely, the spaces of multilinear maps are given by

P^{!}_{I}(\{L_{i}\},M)\equiv\Hom_{{D}(X)^{!}}(\{L_{i}\},M)=\Hom_{{D}(X)}(\otimes^{!}_{i\in I}L_{i},M)\ .

Definition 3.7
.

A
{\textup{Comm}}^{!}
algebra on
X
is a commutative algebra object in the symmetric monoidal category
({D}(X),\otimes^{!})
. We write
{\textup{Comm}}^{!}(X)={\textup{Alg}}_{{\textup{Comm}}}({D}(X)^{!})
for the category of
{\textup{Comm}}^{!}
algebras.

The endomorphism operad of an object
M\in{D}(X)^{!}
is given concretely by

{\textup{End}}_{{D}(X)^{!}}(M)(n)=P^{!}_{n}(\{M\}_{i=1}^{n},M)=\Hom_{{D}(X)}(M^{\otimes^{!}n},M)\ .

3.2.2.
Lie* algebras

Unlike the
!
-tensor structure, the
*
-tensor structure does not arise from a monoidal structure on
{D}(X)
.

Definition 3.8
.

Let
I\in{\textup{fSet}}
and write
\Delta^{(I)}:X\rightarrow X^{I}
for the diagonal embedding. For any
I
-family
L_{i}\in{D}(X)
,
i\in I
, and
M\in{D}(X)
, the multilinear operations in
{D}(X)^{*}
are given by

P^{*}(\{L_{i}\},M)\equiv\Hom_{{D}(X)^{*}}(\{L_{i}\},M)=\Hom_{{D}^{r}(X^{I})}({{\boxtimes}}_{i\in I}L_{i}^{r},\Delta^{I}_{*}M^{r})\ ,

with composition defined as in 2.2.3 of
[
BD04
]
, noting that
\Delta^{I}_{*}
is exact with respect to the right
t
-structures on
{\textup{D}}(X)
and
{\textup{D}}(X^{I})
.

Definition 3.9
.

A
{\textup{Lie}}^{*}
algebra is a Lie algebra,
L\in{\textup{Alg}}_{{\textup{Lie}}}({D}(X)^{*})
internal to the operad
{D}(X)^{*}
.

Concretely, for
b\in\Hom_{{D}(X)^{*}}(\{L,L\},L)
, the composition
b\circ(b\otimes\mathbbm{1})\in\Hom_{{D}(X)^{*}}(\{L,L,L\},L)
is defined by

L{{\boxtimes}}L{{\boxtimes}}L\xrightarrow{b\,{{\boxtimes}}\mathbbm{1}}\Delta_{*}L{{\boxtimes}}L\cong\Delta^{12,3}_{*}(L{{\boxtimes}}L)\xrightarrow{\Delta^{12,3}_{*}(b)}\Delta^{12,3}_{X}\Delta^{(2)}_{*}L=\Delta^{(3)}_{*}L\penalty\ ,

where
\Delta^{12,3}:X^{2}\to X^{3}
is defined by
(x,y)\mapsto(x,x,y)
.

The endomorphism operad of an object
M\in{D}(X)^{*}
is given concretely by

{\textup{End}}_{{D}(X)^{*}}(M)(n)=P_{n}^{*}(\{M\}_{i=1}^{n},M)=\Hom_{{D}(X^{n})}(M^{\boxtimes n},\Delta^{(n)}_{*}M)\ .

3.2.3.
Chiral algebras

For any finite set
I\in{\textup{fSet}}
, let
\Delta^{(I)}:X\to X^{I}
once again denote the diagonal embedding and let

j^{(I)}:U^{(I)}\to X^{I}\qquad\text{for}\qquad U^{(I)}=\{(x_{i})\in X^{I}\ |\ x_{i}\neq x_{j}\text{ for all }i\neq j\}

denote the inclusion of the complement of the union of all diagonals:

Definition 3.10
.

The
chiral
pseudo-tensor structure on
{D}(X)
, denoted as
{D}(X)^{\rm ch}
, is defined as having multilinear operations

P^{\rm ch}_{I}(\{L_{i}\},M)=\Hom_{{D}(X)^{\rm ch}}(\{L_{i}\},M)=\Hom_{{D}^{r}(X^{I})}(j^{(I)}_{*}j^{(I),*}({{\boxtimes}}_{i\in I}L_{i}^{r}),\Delta^{(I)}_{*}M^{r})\penalty\ ,

for
L_{i},M\in{D}(X)
, noting that
\Delta^{(I)}_{*}
,
j^{(I),*}
and
j^{(I)}_{*}
are all exact with respect to the right
t
-structures on
{\textup{D}}(X)
,
{\textup{D}}(X^{I})
and
{\textup{D}}(U^{(I)})
.

There are natural composition maps on the spaces of chiral operations defined in 1.3.2 of
[
BD04
]
.
For example, for
A\in{D}(X)
and
\mu\in\Hom_{{D}(X)^{\rm ch}}(\{A,A\},A)
, the composition
\mu\circ(\mu\otimes\mathbbm{1})\in\Hom_{{D}(X)^{\rm ch}}(\{A,A,A\},A)
is defined by

\displaystyle j^{(3)}_{*}(j^{(3)})^{*}(A{{\boxtimes}}A{{\boxtimes}}A)

\displaystyle=j^{12,3}_{*}j^{12,3*}(j_{*}j^{*}(A{{\boxtimes}}A){{\boxtimes}}A)\xrightarrow{j^{12,3}_{*}j^{12,3*}(\mu{{\boxtimes}}\mathbbm{1})}j^{12,3}_{*}j^{12,3*}(\Delta_{*}(A){{\boxtimes}}A)

\displaystyle\quad\quad=\Delta^{12,3}_{*}(j_{*}j^{*}(A{{\boxtimes}}A))\xrightarrow{\Delta^{12,3}_{*}(\mu)}\Delta^{12,3}_{*}\Delta^{(2)}_{*}A=\Delta^{(3)}_{*}A

where we let
\Delta^{12,3}:X^{2}\to X^{3}
defined by
(x,y)\mapsto(x,x,y)
,
U^{12,3}=\{(x,y,z)|x,y\neq z\}
and
j^{12,3}:U^{12,3}{\ \hookrightarrow\ }X^{3}
.

Definition 3.11
.

A chiral algebra is a Lie algebra
A\in{\textup{Alg}}_{{\textup{Lie}}}({D}(X)^{\rm ch})
internal to the operad
{D}(X)^{\rm ch}
.

Evidently a non-unital chiral algebra structure is equivalent to specifying a map

\mu\in P_{2}^{\rm ch}(\{A,A\},A)=\Hom_{{D}(X^{2})}(j^{*}j_{*}(A{{\boxtimes}}A),\Delta_{*}A)

which is skew-symmetric and satisfies the appropriately defined Jacobi identity.

Definition 3.12
.

A unital chiral algebra is a chiral algebra
A
together with a map
u:\omega_{X}\to A
such that the diagram

(3.1)

\textstyle{j_{*}j^{*}(A\boxtimes\omega_{X})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\Delta_{*}A\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{j_{*}j^{*}(A\boxtimes A)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\Delta_{*}A}

and its higher arity analogues commute, where the left vertical map is that induced by the map
u
, and the bottom horizontal map is given by the chiral multiplication, and the top horizontal map is given by the boundary map in the exact triangle for a complimentary open and closed embedding:

j_{*}j^{*}(A\boxtimes\omega_{X})\to\Delta_{*}\Delta^{!}(A\boxtimes\omega_{X})=\Delta_{*}(A\otimes^{!}\omega_{X})=\Delta_{*}A\ .

Definition 3.13
.

A chiral algebra
A\in{\textup{Alg}}^{\rm ch}(X)
is called commutative if the composition

A{{\boxtimes}}A\to j_{*}j^{*}(A{{\boxtimes}}A)\xrightarrow{\mu}\Delta_{*}A

vanishes, where the first map is the unit of the
(j^{*},j_{*})
-adjunction and the second is the chiral algebra structure map
\mu
. A unital chiral algebra
A\in{\textup{Alg}}_{\textup{un}}^{\rm ch}(X)
is called commutative if it is commutative as a non-unital chiral algebra.

Proposition 3.14
.

There is a natural equivalence of categories between the full subcategory of commutative chiral algebras and
{\textup{Comm}}^{!}
algebras on
X
,

{\textup{Alg}}^{\rm ch}(X)^{\textup{Comm}}\xrightarrow{\cong}{\textup{Alg}}_{\textup{Comm}}({D}(X)^{!})

identifying the respective forgetful functors to
{D}(X)
.

Remark 3.15
.

There is a natural map of operads
{D}(X)^{\rm ch}\to{D}(X)^{*}
which is the identity on objects and arity 1 morphisms, and defined on higher arity morphisms by the maps

\Hom_{{D}(X^{I})}(j^{(I)}_{*}j^{(I),*}({{\boxtimes}}_{i\in I}M_{i}),\Delta^{(I)}_{*}L)\to\Hom_{{D}(X^{I})}({{\boxtimes}}_{i\in I}M_{i},\Delta^{(I)}_{*}L)

induced by

\quad\quad\boxtimes_{i\in I}M_{i}\to j^{(I)}_{*}j^{(I),*}({{\boxtimes}}_{i\in I}M_{i})\ ,

where the latter are given by the unit of the
(j^{(I),*},j^{(I)}_{*})
adjunction. This in turn gives a forgetful functor
{\textup{Alg}}_{{\textup{Lie}}}({D}(X)^{\rm ch})\rightarrow{\textup{Alg}}_{{\textup{Lie}}}({D}(X)^{*})

Proposition 3.16

(
[
BD04
]
)
.

The forgetful functor
{\textup{Alg}}_{{\textup{Lie}}}({D}(X)^{!})\rightarrow{\textup{Alg}}_{{\textup{Lie}}}({D}(X)^{*})
has a left adjoint

{\textup{Alg}}_{{\textup{Lie}}}({D}(X)^{*})\xrightarrow{{\mathcal{V}}}{\textup{Alg}}_{{\textup{Lie}}}({D}(X)^{\rm ch})\penalty\ .

called the chiral envelope.

One should compare this to the free-forgetful adjunction
{\textup{Lie}}(\Vect_{\mathbb{K}})\leftrightarrows{\textup{Ass}}(\Vect_{\mathbb{K}})
recalled in
2.3.3
.

3.3.
Quasicoherent sheaves on
\mathcal{D}_{X}
-schemes

Definition 3.17
.

A
\mathcal{D}_{X}
-scheme is a scheme
\mathcal{Y}
over
X
together with a flat Ehresmann connection on
\mathcal{Y}
over
X
.

The forgetful functor
{D}(X)\to\QC(X)
is symmetric monoidal, so every
{\textup{Comm}}^{!}
algebra
R
on
X
determines a commutative algebra object
R
in
\QC(X)
, or equivalently a scheme
\mathcal{Y}={\textup{Spec\ }}R
that is affine over
X
. The additional data of the
{D}
-module structure on
R
determines a flat connection on
\mathcal{Y}
over
X
. Thus, every
{\textup{Comm}}^{!}
algebra on
X
determines a
\mathcal{D}_{X}
-scheme, and such
\mathcal{D}_{X}
-schemes are called affine.

For
\underline{Y}\to X
a scheme over
X
, we define the
\mathcal{D}_{X}
-scheme of jets
\mathcal{J}(\underline{Y})
of sections of
\underline{Y}
by the property that for any
\mathcal{D}_{X}
-scheme
\mathcal{Z}
, we have

\Hom_{\mathcal{D}_{X}-{\textup{Sch}}}(\mathcal{Z},\mathcal{J}(\underline{Y}))=\Hom_{{\textup{Sch}}_{/X}}(\mathcal{Z},\underline{Y})\ .

In particular, taking
\mathcal{Z}=X
we obtain the familiar property
\Gamma_{\nabla}(X,\mathcal{J}(\underline{Y}))=\Gamma(X,\underline{Y})
. In the case
\underline{Y}=Y\times X
for
Y
a
\mathbb{K}
-scheme, we write simply
\mathcal{J}(Y)
in place of
\mathcal{J}(\underline{Y})
.

In particular, if
\underline{Y}={\textup{Spec\ }}B
for
B
a commutative algebra object in
\QC(X)
, so that
\underline{Y}
is affine over
X
, then we can equivalently define the jet algebra
\mathcal{J}(B)\in{\textup{Comm}}^{!}(X)
by the property

(3.2)

\Hom_{{\textup{Comm}}^{!}(X)}(\mathcal{J}(B),R)=\Hom_{{\textup{Comm}}(\QC(X))}(B,R)\ .

for any
R\in{\textup{Comm}}^{!}(X)
and we have that
\mathcal{J}(\underline{Y})={\textup{Spec\ }}\mathcal{J}(B)
.

3.3.1.

Let
R
be a commutative algebra object in
{D}(X)^{!}
and let
{D}(R)=R{\textup{-Mod}}({D}(X)^{!})
(respectively,
{\textup{D}}(R)={\textup{D}}(R{\textup{-Mod}}({D}(X)^{!}))
) denote the (derived) category of (complexes of)
R
-module objects internal to
{D}(X)
. Equivalently, we let
R[\mathcal{D}_{X}]=R^{l,\heartsuit}\otimes_{\mathcal{O}_{X}}\mathcal{D}_{X}
denote the sheaf of algebras given by differential operators with coefficients in the
{\textup{Comm}}^{!}
algebra
R
. We recall that
{D}(R)
(respectively,
{\textup{D}}(R)
) is equivalent to the (derived) category of (complexes of) sheaves of modules over
R[\mathcal{D}_{X}]
which (have cohomology sheaves that) are quasicoherent as
\mathcal{O}_{X}
modules.

The forgetful functor
{D}(X)\to\QC(X)
is symmetric monoidal, so it induces a natural forgetful functor

{D}(R)=R{\textup{-Mod}}({D}(X)^{!})\to R{\textup{-Mod}}(\QC(X))\ ,

where in the latter case we view
R
as an algebra object in
\QC(X)
, and we let
\mathcal{Y}={\textup{Spec\ }}_{\mathcal{D}_{X}}R
denote the affine D-scheme corresponding to
R
, noting
R{\textup{-Mod}}(\QC(X))=\QC(\mathcal{Y})
the category of quasicoherent sheaves on the underlying scheme of
\mathcal{Y}
. Thus, geometrically we can view
{D}(R)
as the category of quasicoherent sheaves on
\mathcal{Y}
equipped with a compatible flat connection along
X
, and
{\textup{D}}(R)
its derived category.

3.3.2.

Let
\alpha:P\to R
be a map of
{\textup{Comm}}^{!}
algebras on
X
,
\mathcal{X}={\textup{Spec\ }}_{\mathcal{D}_{X}}R
,
\mathcal{Y}={\textup{Spec\ }}_{\mathcal{D}_{X}}P
, and denote the corresponding map of D-schemes by
f:\mathcal{X}\to\mathcal{Y}
. We obtain functors

\mathbb{L}f^{\bullet}:{\textup{D}}(P)\to{\textup{D}}(R)\qquad f^{\bullet}M=R\otimes^{!}_{P}M\qquad\text{and}\qquad\mathbb{R}f_{\bullet}:{\textup{D}}(R)\to{\textup{D}}(P)\qquad f_{\bullet}(M)={\textup{ob}}_{R}^{P}(M)

where
{\textup{ob}}_{R}^{P}(M)\in{\textup{D}}(P)
denotes the functor induced by precomposition with the map
\alpha
, preserving the forgetful functors to
{D}(X)
.

Let
P,R
be
{\textup{Comm}}^{!}
algebras on
X
, and note that
P\boxtimes R
is naturally a
{\textup{Comm}}^{!}
algebra on
X^{2}
. Further, given
M\in{D}(P)
and
N\in{D}(R)
(or
M\in{\textup{D}}(P)
and
N\in{\textup{D}}(R)
), we obtain objects
M\boxtimes N\in{D}(P\boxtimes R)
(or
M\boxtimes N\in{\textup{D}}(P\boxtimes R)
). Similarly,
P\otimes^{!}R
defines a
{\textup{Comm}}^{!}
algebra on
X
, and the functors
\Delta^{!,\heartsuit}:{D}(X^{2})\to{D}(X)
and
\Delta^{!}:{\textup{D}}(X^{2})\to{D}(X)
determine canonical functors

\Delta^{!,\heartsuit}:{D}(P\boxtimes R)\to{D}(P\otimes^{!}R)\qquad\text{and}\qquad\Delta^{!}:{\textup{D}}(P\boxtimes R)\to{\textup{D}}(P\otimes^{!}R)\ .

3.3.3.

The multiplication structure map
m:R\otimes^{!}R\to R
is a map of
{\textup{Comm}}^{!}
algebras, and we denote by
\Delta_{R}:\mathcal{Y}\to\mathcal{Y}^{\times 2}
the corresponding diagonal embedding for
\mathcal{X}={\textup{Spec\ }}_{\mathcal{D}_{X}}R
.

The derived category
{\textup{D}}(R)
is symmetric monoidal with respect to the derived tensor product

M\otimes_{R}^{\mathbb{L},!}N=\mathbb{L}\Delta_{R}^{\bullet}\Delta^{!}(M\boxtimes N)=\mathbb{L}\Delta_{R}^{\bullet}(M\otimes^{\mathbb{L},!}N)\ .

Geometrically, this is simply the usual derived tensor product of quasicoherent sheaves on
\mathcal{Y}
, with its natural tensor product D-module structure.

The abelian category
{D}(R)
is also symmetric monoidal with respect to the non-derived tensor product

M\otimes^{!}_{R}N=\Delta_{R}^{\bullet}\Delta^{!,\heartsuit}(M\boxtimes N)=\Delta_{R}^{\bullet}(M\otimes^{!}N)\ .

Note that for
M\in{D}(R)
projective we have
M\otimes^{!}_{R}N\cong M\otimes_{R}^{\mathbb{L},!}N[d_{X}]
.

3.3.4.

The symmetric monoidal structure
\otimes_{R}^{!}
on
{D}(R)
defined in
3.3.3
above determines a coloured operad structure on
{D}(R)
as usual:

P^{!}_{I,R}(\{L_{i}\},M)=\Hom_{{D}(R)}(\otimes_{R,i\in I}^{!}L_{i},M)\ .

3.3.5.

Next, we define the simplest analogue of the
*
-operations on
{D}(R)
:

Definition 3.18
.

The spaces of
R
-linear
*
-operations are defined by

P_{I,R}^{*}(\{L_{i}\},M)=\Hom_{{D}(R^{\boxtimes I})}(\boxtimes_{i\in I}L_{i},\Delta_{*}M)\subset P_{I}^{*}(\{L_{i}\},M)=\Hom_{{D}(X^{I})}(\boxtimes_{i\in I}L_{i},\Delta_{*}M)\ ,

the subspace of
I
-ary
*
-operations in
{D}(X)
which are
R
-linear in the argument
L_{i}
for each
i\in I
, where we note that the object
\Delta_{*}M\in{D}(X^{I})
inherits an
R^{\boxtimes I}
module structure via

R^{\boxtimes I}\otimes^{!}\Delta_{*}M\cong\Delta_{*}\Delta^{!}(R^{\boxtimes I}\otimes^{!}\Delta_{*}M)\cong R^{\otimes^{!}I}\otimes^{!}M\xrightarrow{m}M\ .

Concretely, a map
\varphi:\boxtimes_{i\in I}L_{i}\to\Delta_{*}M
is
R
-linear in the
i_{0}^{th}
argument if the rightmost square of the diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{i_{0}}^{!}R\otimes(\boxtimes_{i\in I}L_{i})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(R\otimes L_{i_{0}})\boxtimes(\boxtimes_{i\neq i_{0}}L_{i})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\boxtimes_{i\in I}L_{i}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{i_{0}}^{!}R\otimes\Delta_{*}(M)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Delta_{*}(R\otimes M)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Delta_{*}M}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbbm{1}_{\pi_{i_{0}}^{!}R}\otimes\varphi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces m_{L_{0}}\boxtimes\mathbbm{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbbm{1}_{R}\otimes_{i_{0}}\varphi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Delta_{*}m_{M}}

commutes,

where we define
\mathbbm{1}_{R}\otimes_{i_{0}}\varphi:(R\otimes L_{i_{0}})\boxtimes(\boxtimes_{i\neq i_{0}}L_{i})\to\Delta_{*}(R\otimes M)
so that the left square commutes, and
m_{M}:R\otimes M\to M
and
m_{L_{0}}:R\otimes L_{0}\to L_{0}
are the
R
-module structure maps.

Proposition 3.19
.

The spaces of
R
-linear
*
-operations define a coloured operad structure on
{D}(R)
.

It will sometimes be necessary to consider the following more general notion of
R
-polydifferential
*
-operations on
{D}(R)
:

Definition 3.20
.

We define the pseudo-tensor structure of
R
-polydifferential
*
-operations on
{D}(R)
,

P_{I,R-{\textup{Diff}}}^{*}(\{L_{i}\},M)\subset P_{I}^{*}(\{L_{i}\},M)\ ,

as in Section 1.4.8 of
[
BD04
]
.

Note that we have

P_{I,R}^{*}(\{L_{i}\},M)\subset P_{I,R-{\textup{Diff}}}^{*}(\{L_{i}\},M)

as the subspace of all
R
-polydifferential
*
-operations that are supported strictly on the diagonal scheme-theoretically.

Let
M\in{D}(R)
with structure map
m_{M}:R\otimes M\to M
.

Definition 3.21
.

The space of derivations
{\textup{Der}}(R,M)\subset\Hom_{{D}(X)}(R,M)
is the subspace of maps of D-modules
f:R\to M
such that

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces R\otimes R}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces R}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces R\otimes M}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces m}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbbm{1}_{R}\otimes f+\sigma(f\otimes\mathbbm{1}_{R})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces m_{M}}

commutes.

In fact, the functor
{\textup{Der}}(R,\cdot):{D}(R)\to\Vect
is representable by an object
\Omega_{R}\in{D}(R)
equipped with a universal derivation
d_{R}:R\to\Omega_{R}
, called the cotangent sheaf and de Rham differential for
R
, respectively; see 1.4.16 in
[
BD04
]
.

We define the tangent sheaf of
R
(when it exists, for example if
R
is smooth in the sense of 2.3.15 in
[
BD04
]
) to be the dual object

\Theta_{R}=\Omega_{R}^{\circ_{R}}={\mathcal{H}\textup{om}}_{{D}(R)}(\Omega_{R},R)\ \in{D}(R)\ ,

in the sense of Definition
8.22
, which we postpone until Section
8
where these results are primarily used.

Proposition 3.22
.

Suppose
R
is a very smooth
{\textup{Comm}}^{!}
algebra and
X
is affine. Then

h(\Theta_{R})=\Hom_{{D}(R)}(\Omega_{R},R)={\textup{Der}}(R,R)\ .

3.4.
{\textup{Lie}}^{*}
algebroids on
\mathcal{D}_{X}
-schemes

Following 1.4.9 in
[
BD04
]
. Let
L
be a
{\textup{Lie}}^{*}
algebra:

Definition 3.23
.

An action of
L
on
R
by derivations is an
L
-module structure
\tau:L\boxtimes R\to\Delta_{*}R
on the underlying
D
-module of
R
, such that the structure maps
m:R\otimes R\to R
and
u:\omega_{X}\to R
are maps of
L
-modules, that is

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces L\boxtimes(R\otimes R)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces L\boxtimes R}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Delta_{*}(R\otimes R)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Delta_{*}R}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbbm{1}_{L}\boxtimes m}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbbm{1}_{R}\otimes\tau+\sigma(\tau\otimes\mathbbm{1}_{R})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tau}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Delta_{*}m}

and

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces L\boxtimes\omega_{X}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces L\boxtimes R}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Delta_{*}(\omega_{X})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Delta_{*}R}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbbm{1}_{L}\boxtimes u}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tau_{\textup{triv}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tau}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Delta_{*}u}

commute.

Example 3.24
.

(
[
BD04
]
1.4.16) There is a canonical
{\textup{Lie}}^{*}
structure on
\Theta_{R}
determined by the condition that
\Theta_{R}
admits an action on
R
by derivations
\tau_{\textup{univ}}:\Theta_{R}\boxtimes R\to\Delta_{*}R
defined by the element

\mathbbm{1}_{\Omega_{R}}\in{\textup{End}}_{R}(\Omega_{R})={\textup{Der}}(R,\Omega_{R})\subset\Hom_{{D}(X)}(R,\Omega_{R})\cong P_{2}^{\circ!}(R,\{\Omega_{R},R\})\cong P_{2}^{*}(\{\Theta_{R},R\},R)\ ,

or equivalently the universal derivation
d\in{\textup{Der}}(R,\Omega_{R})
, under the above identifications.

Proposition 3.25
.

An action of
L
on
R
by derivations is equivalent to a
{\textup{Lie}}^{*}
map
L\to\Theta_{R}
.

Proof.

Evidently a
{\textup{Lie}}^{*}
map
L\to\Theta_{R}
determines an action of
L
on
R
by derivations, by composition with the universal action
\tau_{\textup{univ}}
. Conversely, given an action of
L
on
R
by derivations, each
l\in\Gamma(X,L)
induces an endomorphism
\tau(l,\cdot)\in{\textup{Der}}(R,R)\subset\Hom_{{D}(X)}(R,R)
and this lifts to a map of
{\textup{Lie}}^{*}
algebras
L\to\Theta_{R}
.
∎

Proposition 3.26
.

An action of
\mathcal{L}
on
R
by derivations is
R
-differential in the second argument, in the sense of Definition
3.20
.

Proof.

By definition a derivation is supported scheme-theoretically on the first-order infinitesimal thickening of the diagonal in
\mathcal{Y}\times\mathcal{Y}
, and thus supported set-theoretically on the diagonal.
∎

Fix an action
\tau
of
L
on
R
by derivations and let
M\in{D}(R)
.

Definition 3.27
.

An action of
L
on
M
compatible with
\tau
is an
L
-module structure
\rho:L\boxtimes M\to\Delta_{*}M
such that the structure map
R\otimes M\to M
is a map of
L
-modules, where
R
is equipped with the
L
-module structure determined by
\tau
, that is

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces L\boxtimes(R\otimes M)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces L\boxtimes M}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Delta_{*}(R\otimes M)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Delta_{*}M}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbbm{1}_{L}\boxtimes m}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbbm{1}_{R}\otimes\rho+\tau\otimes\mathbbm{1}_{M}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\rho}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Delta_{*}m}

commutes.

Definition 3.28
.

(
[
BD04
]
1.4.11)
A
{\textup{Lie}}^{*}
algebroid on
{\textup{Spec\ }}R
is an
R
-module
\mathcal{L}\in{D}(R)
, with

•

a
{\textup{Lie}}^{*}
bracket
b:\mathcal{L}\boxtimes\mathcal{L}\to\Delta_{*}\mathcal{L}
, and

•

an action
\tau:\mathcal{L}\boxtimes R\to\Delta_{*}R
of
\mathcal{L}
on
R
by derivations,

such that

•

\tau
is
R
-linear in the first argument
\mathcal{L}
, in the sense of Definition
3.18
, and

•

the adjoint action
b:\mathcal{L}\boxtimes\mathcal{L}\to\mathcal{L}
is
\tau
-compatible, in the sense of Definition
3.27
.

For example, the tangent sheaf
\Theta_{R}
is naturally a
{\textup{Lie}}^{*}
-algebroid on
R
, and by Proposition
3.25
, every

We note that the structure maps
b
and
\tau
are not
R
-linear in general, but they are necessarily
R
-polydifferential:

Proposition 3.29
.

Let
\mathcal{L}
be a
{\textup{Lie}}^{*}
algebroid on
R
. Then the structure maps
b:\mathcal{L}\boxtimes\mathcal{L}\to\Delta_{*}\mathcal{L}
and
\tau:\mathcal{L}\boxtimes R\to\Delta_{*}R
are
R
-polydifferential in the sense of Definition
3.20
, that is,

b\in P_{2,R-{\textup{Diff}}}^{*}(\{\mathcal{L},\mathcal{L}\},\mathcal{L})\quad\quad\text{and}\quad\quad\tau\in P_{2,R-{\textup{Diff}}}^{*}(\{\mathcal{L},R\},\mathcal{R})\ .

Proof.

The proof is the same as that for Lie algebroids. The map
\tau
is by definition
R
-linear and thus
R
-differential in the first argument, and is
R
-differential in the second argument by Proposition
3.26
. The map
b
is
R
-differential in the second argument, and equivalently the first argument by skew-commutativity, by the formula provided by the
\tau
-compatibility of the adjoint action: the latter is equivalent to commutativity of the diagram
