This is precisely the same diagram of Equation
8.3
which characterized the differential
\tilde{d}_{b}
on
\mathcal{C}^{\bullet}_{{\textup{PV}}}
, and thus we obtain the desired result.
∎

Definition 8.41
.

A coisson structure on
R
is called symplectic if the induced map
\Omega^{1}_{R}\xrightarrow{\cong}\Theta_{R}
of
R
modules internal to
{\mathcal{D}}(X)^{!}
is an isomorphism.

Example 8.42
.

The natural coisson structure on
\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{J}Y}
for
Y
a symplectic variety is symplectic.

Proposition 8.43
.

Let
R
be a symplectic coisson algebra. There is a canonical isomorphism of complexes

\mathcal{C}_{{\textup{PV}}}^{\bullet}(R)\cong\mathcal{C}^{\bullet}_{{\textup{CE}},R}(\Theta_{R})=(\Omega^{\bullet\geq 1}_{{\mathcal{Y}}/X},d_{{\mathcal{Y}}}^{\textup{dR}})[1]\ .

Proof.

The anchor map is a map of
{\textup{Lie}}^{*}
algebras, so by the symplectic hypothesis we have an isomorphism of
{\textup{Lie}}^{*}
algebroids
\Omega^{1}_{R}\xrightarrow{\cong}\Theta_{R}
. Thus, the result follows from Proposition
8.40
.

∎

8.6.
Comparison of relative and absolute de Rham cohomology

Let
X
be a smooth variety of dimension
d_{X}
and recall from Section
3.1
that pushforward along the map
\pi:X\to{\textup{pt}}
defines defines a functor
\pi_{*}:{\textup{D}}(X)\to{\textup{D}}({\textup{pt}})={\textup{D}}({\mathbb{K}}{\textup{-Mod}})
which for
M\in{\textup{D}}^{r}(X)
is computed by

\pi_{*}M=\mathbb{R}\pi_{\bullet}(M^{r}\otimes^{\mathbb{L}}_{\mathcal{D}_{X}}\mathcal{O}_{X})\cong\mathbb{R}\pi_{\bullet}(M^{\ell}\otimes_{\mathcal{O}_{X}}\Omega^{\bullet}_{X})[2d_{X}]

where
M^{\ell}\otimes_{\mathcal{O}_{X}}\Omega^{\bullet}_{X}\in{\textup{D}}({\textup{Sh}}_{\textup{z}}(X))
denotes the algebraic de Rham complex with coefficients in the complex
M^{\ell}
. For
M\in{D}(X)
, this gives

\pi_{*}M=\mathbb{R}\pi_{\bullet}(M^{r}\otimes^{\mathbb{L}}_{\mathcal{D}_{X}}\mathcal{O}_{X})\cong\mathbb{R}\pi_{\bullet}(M^{\ell,\heartsuit}\otimes_{\mathcal{O}_{X}}\Omega^{\bullet}_{X})[d_{X}]

where
M^{\ell,\heartsuit}\otimes_{\mathcal{O}_{X}}\Omega^{\bullet}_{X}\in{\textup{D}}({\textup{Sh}}_{\textup{z}}(X))
denotes the algebraic de Rham complex with coefficients in the left D-module
M^{\ell,\heartsuit}
.

We define the de Rham functor sheaf functor
{\textup{dR}}_{X}:{\textup{D}}(X)\to{\textup{D}}({\textup{Sh}}_{\textup{z}}(X))
by

{\textup{dR}}_{X}(M)=M^{\ell}\otimes_{\mathcal{O}_{X}}\Omega^{\bullet}_{X}[d_{X}]\ ,

so that for
M\in{D}(X)
and in particular
\mathcal{O}_{X}\in{D}^{\ell}(X)
we have

dR_{X}(M)\cong M^{\ell,\heartsuit}\otimes_{\mathcal{O}_{X}}\Omega^{\bullet}_{X}\qquad\text{and in particular}\qquad{\textup{dR}}_{X}(\mathcal{O}_{X})=\Omega^{\bullet}_{X}\ .

Similarly, we define the middle de Rham cohomology sheaf as the functor of abelian categories

\underline{h}:{D}(X)\to{\textup{Sh}}_{\textup{z}}(X)\qquad\text{by}\qquad\underline{h}(M)=M^{r}\otimes_{\mathcal{D}_{X}}\mathcal{O}_{X}\cong M^{r}/(M^{r}\cdot\theta_{X})

for
M=M^{r}\in{D}(X)\cong{D}^{r}(X)
, so that we have
h=\pi_{\bullet}\circ\underline{h}
.

We have the following analogue of Proposition
3.6
in this setting:

Corollary 8.44
.

Let
M\in{D}(X)
be a locally projective. Then

{\textup{dR}}_{X}(M)\xrightarrow{\cong}\underline{h}(M)[-d_{X}]\ .

8.6.1.

Throughout the remainder of this section, let
X
be a smooth curve,
R
a very smooth
{\textup{Comm}}^{!}
algebra on
X
, and
\mathcal{Y}={\textup{Spec\ }}_{\mathcal{D}_{X}}R
the corresponding affine D-scheme on
X
, a (pro-finite-type) variety affine over
X
equipped with a flat Ehresmann connection relative to
X
.

Following 2.8.11 of
[
BD04
]
, we let
\Omega^{\bullet}_{\mathcal{Y}/X}\in{\textup{D}}(X)
denote the complex of D-modules

\Omega^{\bullet}_{\mathcal{Y}/X}=\left[R\xrightarrow{d^{\textup{dR}}_{R}}\Omega^{1}_{R}[-1]\xrightarrow{d^{\textup{dR}}_{R}}\Omega^{2}_{R}[-2]\to\cdots\right]\ .

The notation
\Omega_{\mathcal{Y}/X}^{\bullet}
is chosen because the underlying complex of Zariski sheaves on
\mathcal{Y}
is naturally identified with the usual relative algebraic de Rham complex of
\mathcal{Y}\to X
. Note that while each cohomologically graded summand of the complex is an object of
{D}(R)
, this does not define an object of
{\textup{D}}(R)
since the maps are not
R
-linear, analogous to the fact that the usual de Rham complex
\Omega^{\bullet}_{X}\in{\textup{D}}({\textup{Sh}}_{\textup{z}}(X))
is not a complex of quasicoherent sheaves on
X
, despite that fact that each of its analogous summands are objects of
\QC(X)
.

Let
\Omega^{\bullet}_{\mathcal{Y}}\in{D}({\textup{Sh}}_{\textup{z}}(X))
denote the absolute de Rham complex of
\mathcal{Y}
, and note that the Ehresmann connection on
\mathcal{Y}\xrightarrow{\pi_{\mathcal{Y}}}X
determines by definition a splitting

\Omega^{1}_{\mathcal{Y}}\cong\Omega^{1}_{\mathcal{Y}/X}\oplus\pi_{\mathcal{Y}}^{\bullet}\Omega^{1}_{X}\qquad\text{and thus}\qquad\Omega^{k}_{\mathcal{Y}}\cong\Omega^{k}_{\mathcal{Y}/X}\oplus(\Omega^{k-1}_{\mathcal{Y}/X}\otimes_{\mathcal{O}_{Y}}\pi_{\mathcal{Y}}^{\bullet}\Omega^{1}_{X})

which together define an isomorphism of complexes of Zariski sheaves on
X

{\textup{dR}}_{X}(\Omega^{\bullet}_{\mathcal{Y}/X})\xrightarrow{\cong}\Omega^{\bullet}_{\mathcal{Y}}\ .

Similarly, we let
\Omega^{\bullet\geq 1}_{\mathcal{Y}/X}\in{\textup{D}}(X)
denote the complex of D-modules given by the stupid truncation at
1
, that is

\Omega^{\bullet\geq 1}_{\mathcal{Y}/X}=\sigma_{1}\Omega^{\bullet}_{\mathcal{Y}/X}=\left[\Omega^{1}_{R}[-1]\xrightarrow{d^{\textup{dR}}_{R}}\Omega^{2}_{R}[-2]\to\cdots\right]\ .

The above isomorphism restricts to an isomorphism of complexes

{\textup{dR}}_{X}(\Omega^{\bullet\geq 1}_{\mathcal{Y}/X})\xrightarrow{\cong}\left[\Omega^{1}_{\mathcal{Y}/X}[-1]\xrightarrow{d^{\textup{dR}}_{\mathcal{Y}}}\Omega^{\bullet\geq 2}_{\mathcal{Y}}\right]\ .

In particular, note that we have the compatible inclusions of complexes

(8.6)

{\textup{dR}}_{X}(\Omega^{\bullet\geq 1}_{\mathcal{Y}/X})\subseteq{\textup{dR}}_{X}(\Omega^{\bullet}_{\mathcal{Y}/X})\xrightarrow{\cong}\Omega^{\bullet}_{\mathcal{Y}}

and thus we obtain a canonical map of complexes

\mathbb{R}\pi_{\bullet}{\textup{dR}}_{X}(\Omega^{\bullet\geq 1}_{\mathcal{Y}/X})\to\mathbb{R}\pi_{\bullet}\Omega^{\bullet}_{\mathcal{Y}}=C^{\bullet}_{\textup{dR}}(\mathcal{Y})\ .

Proposition 8.45
.

Suppose in addition that
R
is locally free as an
\mathcal{O}_{X}
module. Then

{\textup{dR}}_{X}(\Omega^{\bullet\geq 1}_{\mathcal{Y}/X})\cong\underline{h}(\Omega^{\bullet\geq 1}_{\mathcal{Y}/X})[-1]

If in addition
X
is affine, we obtain canonical isomorphisms of objects in
{\textup{D}}({\mathbb{K}}{\textup{-Mod}})

h(\Omega^{\bullet\geq 1}_{\mathcal{Y}/X})[-1]=\pi_{\bullet}\underline{h}(\Omega^{\bullet\geq 1}_{\mathcal{Y}/X})[-1]\cong\mathbb{R}\pi_{\bullet}\underline{h}(\Omega^{\bullet\geq 1}_{\mathcal{Y}/X})[-1]\cong\mathbb{R}\pi_{\bullet}{\textup{dR}}_{X}(\Omega^{\bullet\geq 1}_{\mathcal{Y}/X})\ ,

so that in particular we have a natural map in the derived category

(8.7)

h(\Omega^{\bullet\geq 1}_{\mathcal{Y}/X})[-1]\to\Omega^{\bullet}_{\mathcal{Y}}\ .

Now suppose
\mathcal{Y}=\mathcal{J}(Y)
for
Y
a smooth affine variety and
X=\mathbb{A}^{1}
. We have:

Proposition 8.46
.

There is a natural quasi-isomorphism

C^{\bullet}_{\textup{dR}}(Y)\xrightarrow{\sim}C^{\bullet}_{\textup{dR}}(\mathcal{J}(Y))\ .

Proof.

Let
\mathcal{J}(Y)_{0}=\mathcal{J}(Y)\times_{\mathbb{A}^{1}}\{0\}
be the fiber of
\mathcal{J}(Y)
at the origin, and recall we have a natural identification
\mathcal{J}(Y)_{0}={\textup{Maps}}(\mathbb{D}_{0},Y)
where
\mathbb{D}_{0}=(\mathbb{A}^{1})^{\wedge}_{\{0\}}
denotes the formal disk at the origin. We will construct algebraic homotopy equivalences between
\mathcal{J}(Y)
and
\mathcal{J}(Y)_{0}
, and between
\mathcal{J}(Y)_{0}
and
Y
, so that the claim follows from invariance of algebraic de Rham cohomology of varieties under homotopy equivalences.

Since
Y\times\mathbb{A}^{1}\to\mathbb{A}^{1}
is canonically trivialized as a scheme over
\mathbb{A}^{1}
,
\mathcal{J}(Y)
admits a natural identification
\mathcal{J}(Y)=\mathcal{J}(Y)_{0}\times\mathbb{A}^{1}
, and we let
r_{0}:\mathcal{J}(Y)\to\mathcal{J}(Y)_{0}
denote the induced projection map, which corresponds geometrically to restriction to the origin of an algebraic family of jets of maps to
Y
. We let
\iota_{0}:\mathcal{J}(Y)_{0}\to\mathcal{J}(Y)
denote the natural inclusion, and note that
r_{0}\circ\iota=\mathbbm{1}_{\mathcal{J}(Y)_{0}}
. Further, define the algebraic homotopy

h:\mathbb{A}^{1}\times\mathcal{J}(Y)\to\mathcal{J}(Y)\qquad\text{by}\qquad(t,\varphi_{0},x)\mapsto(\varphi_{0},tx)\ .

Evidently we have
h|_{\{0\}\times\mathcal{J}(Y)}=\iota_{0}\circ r_{0}
and
h|_{\{1\}\times\mathcal{J}(Y)}=\mathbbm{1}_{\mathcal{J}(Y)}
, so that
r_{0}
and
\iota_{0}
define an algebraic homotopy equivalence, as desired.

Let
{\textup{ev}}_{0}:\mathcal{J}(Y)_{0}\to Y
denote the evaluation at the closed point
0\in\mathbb{D}_{0}
, so that
{\textup{ev}}_{0}(\varphi_{0})=\varphi_{0}(0)
, where on the right hand side we have written
\varphi_{0}\in\mathcal{J}(Y)_{0}
in terms of the corresponding map
\varphi_{0}:\mathbb{D}_{0}\to Y
. There is also a canonical constant jet extension
c:Y\to\mathcal{J}(Y)_{0}
mapping a closed point
y\in Y
to the constant jet
\varphi_{y}:\mathbb{D}_{0}\to\{y\}{\ \hookrightarrow\ }Y
. Note that we have
{\textup{ev}}_{0}\circ c=\mathbbm{1}_{Y}
. Further, define the algebraic homotopy

h:\mathbb{A}^{1}\times\mathcal{J}(Y)_{0}\to\mathcal{J}(Y)_{0}\qquad\text{by}\qquad\varphi_{0}\mapsto\varphi_{0}\circ m_{t}

where
m_{t}:\mathbb{D}_{0}\to\mathbb{D}_{0}
denotes the restrition of the multiplicative monoid action
m:\mathbb{A}^{1}\times\mathbb{D}_{0}\to\mathbb{D}_{0}
to
\{t\}\times\mathbb{D}_{0}
. Evidently we have
h|_{\{0\}\times\mathcal{J}(Y)_{0}}=c\circ{\textup{ev}}_{0}
and
h|_{\{1\}\times\mathcal{J}(Y)}=\mathbbm{1}_{\mathcal{J}(Y)_{0}}
, so that
{\textup{ev}}_{0}
and
c
define another algebraic homotopy equivalence, as desired.

∎

We now combine the preceding two propositions to establish the main result of this section:

Proposition 8.47
.

Let
X=\mathbb{A}^{1}
and
\mathcal{Y}=\mathcal{J}(Y)
for
Y
a smooth affine variety. Then the map
h(\Omega^{\bullet\geq 1}_{\mathcal{Y}/X})[-1]\to\Omega^{\bullet}_{\mathcal{Y}}
of Equation
8.7
induces isomorphisms in cohomology

H^{k}(h(\Omega^{\bullet\geq 1}_{\mathcal{Y}/X})[-1])\xrightarrow{\cong}H^{k}_{\textup{dR}}(Y)\qquad\text{for $k\geq 3$.}

Proof.

By Proposition
8.45
, the map of Equation
8.7
is modelled by the inclusion of subcomplexes of Equation
8.6
. This inclusion determines the exact triangle of complexes

{\textup{dR}}_{X}(\Omega^{\bullet\geq 1}_{\mathcal{Y}/X})\to\Omega^{\bullet}_{\mathcal{Y}}\to{\textup{dR}}_{X}(\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{Y}})\ ,

and the cokernel
{\textup{dR}}_{X}(\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{Y}})
is concentrated in cohomological degree
0
and
1
, so that its cohomology is vanishing in degree greater than or equal to
2
. Thus, the inclusion defines an isomorphism in cohomology

H^{k}({\textup{dR}}_{X}(\Omega^{\bullet\geq 1}_{\mathcal{Y}/X}))\xrightarrow{\cong}H^{k}_{\textup{dR}}(\mathcal{Y})

for
k\geq 3
. By Proposition
8.46
, we have
H^{k}_{\textup{dR}}(\mathcal{Y})\cong H^{k}_{\textup{dR}}(Y)
and thus we obtain the desired result.
∎

9.
Examples

In this section, we explain the application of the results of Subsection
7.3
to several families of examples. We begin with the case of chiral quantizations of affine symplectic varieties, applying Theorem
8.1
to establish the chiral analogue of Theorem
7.8
. Next, we return to the various examples from Section
4.3
and describe their deformation theory, culminating in a proof of the rigidity of boundary Virasoro minimal models in Subsection
9.5
.

All the examples we consider are naturally vertex algebras, and thus in terms of D-modules we fix
X=\mathbb{A}^{1}
and work in the weakly translation invariant setting.

9.1.
Chiral quantizations of affine symplectic varieties

Throughout this section, let
Y
be an affine, symplectic variety,
\mathcal{J}Y
the affine D-scheme of jets to
Y
, and recall there is a natural Coisson algebra structure on
\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{J}Y}
, as explained in the beginning of Section
8
. Further, we denote by
\varphi\in{\textup{Alg}}_{{\textup{Lie}}}(P^{\mathbb{G}_{a},c}(\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{J}Y}))
the vertex Poisson algebra structure on
\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{J}Y}
.

We begin by establishing the chiral analogue of Theorem
7.8
:

Theorem 9.1
.

Suppose
\varphi_{\hbar/\hbar^{m}}
is a quantization of
\varphi
defined modulo
\hbar^{m}
. If the obstruction class vanishes, then the space of extensions of
\varphi_{\hbar/\hbar^{m}}
, up to isomorphism,

{\mathfrak{Quant}}^{\rm ch}({\mathbb{K}}[\hbar/\hbar^{m+1}])\times_{{\mathfrak{Quant}}_{\varphi}^{\rm ch}({\mathbb{K}}[\hbar/\hbar^{m}])}\{\varphi_{\hbar/\hbar^{m}}\}

is a torsor for
H^{3}_{{\textup{dR}}}(Y)
, the third de Rham cohomology of
Y
.

Proof.

This follows directly from Theorems
7.26
and
8.1
.
∎

Similarly, by Corollaries
7.27
and
7.28
, together with Theorem
8.1
, we have:

Corollary 9.2
.

Suppose
H^{4}_{\textup{dR}}(Y)=\{0\}
. Then
{\textup{Quant}}^{\rm ch}_{\varphi}({\mathbb{K}}{[\![\hbar]\!]})
is nonempty.

Corollary 9.3
.

Suppose
H^{3}_{\textup{dR}}(Y)=\{0\}
. Then if there exists
\varphi_{\hat{\hbar}}\in{\textup{Quant}}^{\rm ch}_{\varphi}({\mathbb{K}}{[\![\hbar]\!]})
, a formal chiral quantization of
Y
, every such quantization is isomorphic to
\varphi_{\hat{\hbar}}
.

Now suppose
Y
is an affine symplectic variety with an action of
\mathbb{G}_{\hbar}
that scales the symplectic form with unit weight. Then
{\mathcal{O}}(Y)
is a graded Poisson algebra and
{\mathcal{O}}({\mathcal{J}}Y)
is a graded vertex Poisson algebra. The grading on
{\mathcal{O}}(Y)
induces one on
\Omega_{Y}
, and thus on the de Rham cohomology, which we denote
H^{\bullet}_{\textup{dR}}(Y)=\bigoplus_{m\in\mathbb{Z}}H^{\bullet,\langle m\rangle}(Y)
.

Theorem 9.4
.

Suppose
H^{3,\langle m\rangle}_{\textup{dR}}(Y)=0
unless
m=m_{0}\in\mathbb{N}
. Then if there exists
\varphi_{\hbar}\in{\mathfrak{Quant}}^{\rm ch}_{\varphi,{\textup{gr}}}({\mathbb{K}}{[\![\hbar]\!]})
, a graded chiral quantization of
Y
, we have

{\mathfrak{Quant}}^{\rm ch}_{\varphi,{\textup{gr}}}({\mathbb{K}}{[\![\hbar]\!]})\cong H^{3}_{\textup{dR}}(Y)\penalty\ .

9.2.
Chiral differential operators on smooth affine varieties

Let
Y
be a smooth affine variety with vanishing second Chern class, then from the discussion of
4.3.2
we know that for each
\alpha\in\Omega^{3,cl}(Y)
the homogenized cdos
{\mathcal{D}}^{{\rm ch}}_{\hbar,\alpha}(Y)
provide a graded chiral quantization of
T^{*}Y
.

Proposition 9.5

(
[
GMS04
,
GMS03
]
)
.

Suppose
\alpha,\alpha^{\prime}\in\Omega^{3,cl}(Y)
with
\alpha-\alpha^{\prime}=d\eta
for some
\eta\in\Omega^{2}(Y)
, then

{\mathcal{D}}^{\rm ch}_{\hbar,\alpha}(Y)\cong{\mathcal{D}}^{\rm ch}_{\hbar,\alpha^{\prime}}(Y)

Thus, the moduli space of cdos on
Y
, up to equivalence, is a torsor for
H^{3}_{\rm dR}(Y)
.

Proof.

By Proposition
4.28
,
{\rm Fil}({\mathcal{D}}^{\rm ch}_{\hbar,\alpha}(Y))\cong{\mathcal{D}}^{\rm ch}_{\alpha}(Y)
. From
[
GMS04
]
, we know that
{\mathcal{D}}^{\rm ch}_{\alpha}(Y)\cong{\mathcal{D}}^{\rm ch}_{\alpha^{\prime}}(Y)
as ordinary vertex algebras. The result then follows since
{\rm Fil}
reflects isomorphisms by Proposition
4.21
.
∎

Theorem 9.6
.

Let
Y
be a smooth affine variety with vanishing second Chern class. Then
T^{*}Y
admits a graded chiral quantization and any graded chiral quantization of
T^{*}Y
is isomorphic to a vertex algebra of cdos on
Y
.

Proof.

Since the second Chern class of
Y
vanishes, there exists at least one algebra of chiral differential operators on
Y
—which as we recalled in Section
4.3.2
provides a graded chiral quantization of
T^{*}Y
.

Now
H^{3}_{\textup{dR}}(T^{*}Y)\cong H^{3}_{{\textup{dR}}}(Y)
sits in a single
\hbar
-degree, with respect to the
\mathbb{G}_{\hbar}
scaling the symplectic form. Thus, by Corollary
7.31
, the moduli space of chiral quantizations of
T^{*}Y
is a torsor for
H^{3}_{{\textup{dR}}}(Y)
. Since the space of cdos is a torsor for the same group, we have the desired result.
∎

Remark 9.7
.

It is well known that cdos on
Y
exist only if the second Chern class,
c_{2}\in H^{4}_{\rm dR}(Y)
vanishes. We have shown that the obstruction to the existence of a chiral quantization lives in
H^{4}_{\rm dR}(T^{*}Y)\cong H^{4}_{\rm dR}(Y)
, the same cohomology group. While it is natural to ask how the two are related we leave this to future work.

9.3.
Affine current algebras

Let
\mathfrak{g}
be a simple Lie algebra, and let
\mathfrak{g}^{*}
be its linear dual. Recall that for any
k\in{\mathbb{K}}
, the homogenized universal affine vertex algebra
V^{k}_{\hbar}(\mathfrak{g})
is a graded chiral quantization of
\mathfrak{g}^{*}
.

Proposition 9.8
.

The first coisson cohomology group of
{\mathcal{O}}({\mathcal{J}}\mathfrak{g}^{*})
is one-dimensional, i.e.,

H^{1}_{\rm c}({\mathcal{O}}({\mathcal{J}}\mathfrak{g}^{*}))={\mathbb{K}}\penalty\ .

Proof.

Since the underlying
{\textup{Comm}}^{!}
-algebra
{\mathcal{O}}({\mathcal{J}}\mathfrak{g}^{*})
is very smooth, by Corollary
5.45
,

H^{\bullet}_{c}({\mathcal{O}}({\mathcal{J}}\mathfrak{g}^{*}))\cong H^{\bullet}_{\rm PV}({\mathcal{O}}({\mathcal{J}}\mathfrak{g}^{*}))\penalty\ .

From
[
BDSK20
]
,
H^{1}_{\rm PV}({\mathcal{O}}({\mathcal{J}}\mathfrak{g}^{*})))\cong H^{2}_{{\textup{Lie}}}(\mathfrak{g},{\mathbb{K}})_{\oplus}H^{3}_{{\textup{Lie}}}(\mathfrak{g},{\mathbb{K}})
. Since
\mathfrak{g}
is simple,
H^{2}_{{\textup{Lie}}}(\mathfrak{g},{\mathbb{K}})=0
by the Whitehead Lemma and
H^{3}_{{\textup{Lie}}}(\mathfrak{g},{\mathbb{K}})={\mathbb{K}}
.
∎

Theorem 9.9
.

Any graded chiral quantization of
\mathfrak{g}^{*}
is isomorphic to
V^{k}_{\hbar}(\mathfrak{g}^{*})
for some
k\in{\mathbb{K}}
.

Proof.

By Proposition
9.8
, the coisson cohomology is in a single graded degree and so the result follows from Corollary
7.32
.
∎

This is similar to the result of
[
KQ24
]
on the moduli space of first order deformations of affine current algebras, though they use the cohomology theory of
[
Hua14a
]
.

9.4.
The Virasoro algebra

Recall the vertex Poisson algebra
V={\mathcal{J}}{\mathbb{K}}[T]
with
\lambda
-bracket

[T_{\lambda}T]=2\lambda T+\partial T\penalty\ ,

introduced as the classical limit of the Virasoro algebra in
4.3.3
.

Proposition 9.10
.

The vertex Poisson algebra
V
admits a one parameter family of graded quantizations and any such quantization is isomorphic to the homogenized Virasoro algebra,
{\textup{Vir}}^{c}_{\hbar}
, for some
c\in{\mathbb{K}}
.

Proof.

As a
{\textup{Comm}}^{!}
-algebra,
V
is very smooth and so by Corollary
5.45
,

H^{\bullet}_{\rm c}(V)\cong H^{\bullet}_{\rm PV}(V)\penalty\ .

From
[
BDSK20
]
,

H^{1}_{\rm PV}(V)={\mathbb{K}}

and so by Corollary
7.32
, the moduli space of quantisations is isomorphic to
{\mathbb{A}^{1}}
.
∎

9.5.
Boundary minimal models

Recall from
4.3.3
that when
c=c_{2,2n+1}
the homogenized Virasoro algebra develops a vertex ideal
{\mathcal{I}}_{\hbar,n}
defining the boundary minimal model

{\mathcal{M}}_{\hbar,n}={\textup{Vir}}^{c_{2,2n+1}}_{\hbar}/{\mathcal{I}}_{\hbar,n}\penalty\ .

The classical limit of
{\mathcal{M}}_{\hbar,n}
can be identified with the arc algebra
M_{n}={\mathcal{J}}({\mathbb{K}}[T]/T^{n})
, of which it is a graded quantization.

In the rest of this section we shall show that the boundary Virasoro minimal models,
{\mathcal{M}}_{n}
, are rigid,
i.e.
, they admit no deformations.

Unlike the examples we considered previously,
{\mathcal{J}}({\mathbb{K}}[T]/T^{n})
is not smooth, let alone very smooth. As a result, its Harrison cohomology is non-vanishing and, moreover, since
{\mathcal{J}}({\mathbb{K}}[T]/T^{n})
is not symplectic, we cannot relate its variational cohomology to the de Rham cohomology.

9.5.1.

Our main computational tool will be the bicomplex from Proposition
5.39
,
C_{c}^{\bullet,\bullet}(M_{n})
, whose total cohomology is isomorphic to the classical cohomology. The total cohomology can be computed by the first quadrant spectral sequence whose first page is

(9.1)

E_{1}^{p,q}=H_{{\textup{AQ}}}^{q}(C^{p,q}_{c}(M_{n}))\penalty\ .

for
p,q\geq 0
, where we, implicitly, make use of the quasi-isomorphism between the Harrison and André–Quillen cohomologies.

Note that we have the identifications

E_{1}^{0,q}=H^{q}_{{\textup{AQ}}}(M_{n})\penalty\ ,\qquad\text{ and }\qquad E_{2}^{p,0}=C^{\bullet}_{\rm PV}(M_{n})\penalty\ .

The spectral sequence converges to the total cohomology and since it is first quadrant, the first classical cohomology group can be computed by the entries of the third page.

(9.2)

H^{1}_{c}(M_{n})\cong E_{3}^{1,0}\oplus E_{3}^{0,1}=H_{{{\textup{PV}}}}^{1}(M_{n})\oplus E_{3}^{0,1}\penalty\ ,

where we make use of the identification
E_{3}^{1,0}=E_{2}^{1,0}=H_{{{\textup{PV}}}}^{1}(M_{n})
.

We will also make extensive use of André–Quillen and variational cohomology with coefficients not necessarily in the adjoint module. The reader can find a comprehensive, concrete definition of these in
[
BDSH+21
]
but, for the sake of completeness, let us recall some basic definitions.

For
A\in{\textup{Comm}}^{!}({\mathbb{K}}[{{\partial}}]{\textup{-Mod}})
is a commutative
{\mathbb{K}}
-algebra with derivation and
B\in A{\textup{-Mod}}({\mathbb{K}}[{{\partial}}]{\textup{-Mod}})
, a module over
A
, we write

C_{\textup{AQ}}^{\bullet}(A,B)=\Hom_{A{\textup{-Mod}}({\mathbb{K}}[\partial]{\textup{-Mod}})}(\mathbb{L}^{\bullet}_{A},B)\penalty\ ,

where
\mathbb{L}_{A}^{\bullet}
is the cotangent complex of
A
. Moreover,

H^{0}_{{\textup{AQ}}}(A,B)\cong{\textup{Der}}_{{\partial}}(A,B)\penalty\ .

by construction.

Similarly for
A
a vertex Poisson algebra and
B
a vertex Poisson module over
A
, we write

C_{{\textup{PV}}}^{n}(A,B)=\Hom_{\otimes_{i=1}^{n}A[{{\partial}}_{i}]{\textup{-Mod}}}((\Omega_{A}^{1})^{\otimes n},B\otimes_{{\mathbb{K}}[{{\partial}}]}{\mathbb{K}}[\lambda_{1},\dots,\lambda_{n}])\penalty\ ,

with differential
d_{PV}
given by Schouten bracket against the Poisson bracket.

More concretely, a cochain
f_{\lambda_{1},\dots,\lambda_{n}}\in C_{{\textup{PV}}}^{n}(A,B)
is a multilinear
f_{\lambda_{1},\dots,\lambda_{n}}\in\Hom_{\mathbb{K}}(A^{n},B[\lambda_{1},\dots,\lambda_{n}])
such that for any
a_{1},\dots,a_{n},c\in A

(i)

f(a_{1},\dots,\partial a_{i},\dots a_{n})=-\lambda_{i}f(a_{1},\dots,a_{i},\dots,a_{n})\penalty\

(ii)

f(a_{\sigma^{-1}(1)},\dots a_{\sigma^{-1}(n)})=\textup{sgn}(\sigma)f(a_{1},\dots,a_{n})
for any
\sigma\in{{\mathfrak{S}}}_{n}
.

(iii)

f(a_{1},\dots,ca_{i},\dots,a_{n})=cf_{\lambda_{1},\dots,\lambda_{i}+{{\partial}},\dots,\lambda_{n}}(a_{1},\dots,a_{i},\dots,a_{n})_{\leftarrow}+a_{i}f_{\lambda_{1},\dots,\lambda_{i}+{{\partial}},\dots,\lambda_{n}}(a_{1},\dots,c,\dots,a_{n})_{\leftarrow}
, where the subscript
\leftarrow
indicates that
\lambda_{i}+{{\partial}}
acts towards the left.

with differential

\displaystyle(d_{{\textup{PV}}}f)_{\lambda_{1},\dots,\lambda_{n+1}}(a_{1},\dots,a_{n+1})

\displaystyle=(-1)^{n}\sum_{i}(-1)^{i}\bigg[v_{i}\ {}_{\lambda_{i}}f_{\lambda_{1},\dots,\hat{\lambda_{i}},\dots,\lambda_{n+1}}(a_{1},\dots,\hat{a_{i}},\dots,a_{n})\bigg]

\displaystyle+(-1)^{n+1}\sum_{i<j}f_{\lambda_{i}+\lambda_{j},\lambda_{1},\dots,\hat{\lambda_{i}},\dots,\hat{\lambda_{j}},\dots,\lambda_{n+1}}([a_{i}\ {}_{\lambda_{i}}a_{j}],a_{1},\dots,\hat{a_{i}},\dots,\hat{a_{j}},\dots,a_{n+1})\penalty\ ,

where the hat denotes omission.

Breaking with our former conventions, we shall now explicitly denote the module of coefficients in the following.

9.5.2.
The André–Quillen cohomology

We have an inclusion

\iota:C_{{\textup{AQ}}}^{\bullet}(M_{n},M_{n})\rightarrow C^{\bullet}_{{\textup{AQ}}}(V,M_{n})\penalty\ ,

with image equal to the subcomplex of cochains in
C_{{\textup{AQ}}}^{\bullet}(V,M_{n})
that vanish if any argument is in
I_{n}
. The cokernel complex,
{\textup{coker}}(\iota)
, admits a nice description in low degrees. Namely,

{\textup{coker}}^{0}(\iota)\cong\Hom_{{\mathbb{K}}[\partial]}(I_{n},M_{n})\penalty\ ,

The quotient map
C^{0}_{{\textup{AQ}}}(V,M_{n})=\Hom_{\partial}(V,M_{n})\rightarrow\Hom_{\partial}(I_{n},M_{n})
is given by restricting the domain. For
f\in\Hom_{\partial}(I_{n},M_{n})
, the induced differential on the cokernel is

df(a,b)=af(b)+f(a)b-f(ab)\penalty\ ,

for
a,b\in V
. For
f
to be closed, it must vanish on
I_{n}
, and so being closed is equivalent to

f(ab)\overset{!}{=}af(b)

for
a\in V
and
b\in I_{n}
,
i.e.
,
f
is
V
-linear and we can identify

(9.3)

H^{0}({\textup{coker}}(\iota))=\Hom_{V}(I_{n},M_{n})\penalty\ .

The short exact sequence

0\rightarrow C_{{\textup{AQ}}}^{\bullet}(M_{n},M_{n})\rightarrow C_{{\textup{AQ}}}^{\bullet}(V,M_{n})\rightarrow{\textup{coker}}^{\bullet}(\iota)\rightarrow 0

induces the usual long exact sequence in cohomology:

0\rightarrow{\textup{Der}}_{\partial}(M_{n},M_{n})\rightarrow{\textup{Der}}_{\partial}(V,M_{n})\rightarrow H^{0}({\textup{coker}}(\iota))\rightarrow H^{1}_{{\textup{AQ}}}(M_{n},M_{n})\rightarrow 0\rightarrow\dots\penalty\ ,

where we used the fact that
H^{1}_{{\textup{AQ}}}(V,M_{n})=0
since
V
is very smooth.

Thus,

(9.4)

H^{1}_{{\textup{AQ}}}(M_{n},M_{n})\cong H^{0}({\textup{coker}}(\iota))/{\textup{Der}}_{\partial}(V,M_{n})\penalty\ ,

i.e.
, the cocycles in
{\textup{coker}}(\iota)
that do not arise from the restriction of a derivation on
V
. Concretely, for every such
f\in H^{0}({\textup{coker}}(\iota))
,
d_{{\textup{AQ}}}f
is a cocycle in
C^{1}_{{\textup{AQ}}}(M_{n},M_{n})
and
[d_{{\textup{AQ}}}f]\in H^{1}_{{\textup{AQ}}}(M_{n},M_{n})
.

Lemma 9.11
.

The
(0,1)
-entry of the second page of the spectral sequence vanishes, i.e.,

E_{2}^{0,1}=H^{0}_{\rm PV}(H^{1}_{{\textup{AQ}}}(M_{n},M_{n}))=0\penalty\ ,

and so
E_{3}^{0,1}=0
.

Proof.

In this presentation, the kernel of the
E_{1}
-differential
d_{1}^{0,1}:H^{1}_{{\textup{AQ}}}(M_{n},M_{n})\rightarrow H^{1,\langle 1\rangle}_{\rm AQ}(M_{n},M_{n})
admits a nice description. A class
[d_{{\textup{AQ}}}f]\in H^{1}_{{\textup{AQ}}}(M_{n},M_{n})
, with
f\in H^{0}({\textup{coker}}(\iota))
, is closed for this differential if and only if there exists some
f^{\prime}\in C^{0,1}_{\rm c}(M_{n},M_{n})
such that

d_{\rm PV}d_{{\textup{AQ}}}f=d_{{\textup{AQ}}}f^{\prime}\penalty\ .

We will show that
f^{\prime}
exists only if
[d_{{\textup{AQ}}}f]=H^{1}_{{\textup{AQ}}}(M_{n},M_{n})
,
i.e.
, we will show that the kernel of
d_{1}^{0,1}
is trivial.

A naive ansatz for
f^{\prime}
is just
d_{\rm PV}f
since
d_{{\textup{AQ}}}d_{\rm PV}f=-d_{\rm PV}d_{{\textup{AQ}}}f
. However,
d_{\rm PV}f
generically fails to be a well-defined cochain for
M_{n}
since it might fail to vanish on the ideal. Thus, the existence of
f^{\prime}
is equivalent to the existence of a
\delta f\in C^{0,\langle 1\rangle}_{\rm AQ}(V,M_{n})
such that

f^{\prime}=d_{\rm PV}f-\delta f\in C_{\rm AQ}^{0,\langle 1\rangle}(M_{n},M_{n})\penalty\ .

So we want to show that
\delta f
can only exist if
[d_{{\textup{AQ}}}f]=0\in H^{1}_{{\textup{AQ}}}(M_{n},M_{n})
. Since,

d_{{\textup{AQ}}}(d_{\rm PV}f-f^{\prime})=0\penalty\ ,

we can restrict
\delta f\in C^{1}_{\rm PV}(V,M_{n})
. Since
f^{\prime}
must vanish on
I_{n}
, in particular it should vanish when one of the arguments is
T^{n+k}
for
k\geq 0
,
i.e.

f^{\prime}(T,T^{n+k})=(d_{\rm PV}f-\delta f)(T,T^{n+k})\overset{!}{=}0\penalty\ .

But since
\delta f
satisfies the right Leibniz rule,
\delta f(T,T^{n+k})=(n+k)T^{n+k-1}\delta f(T,T)
and we see that

(9.5)

d_{\rm PV}f(T,T^{n+k})\overset{!}{=}\begin{cases}nT^{n-1}\delta f(T,T)\text{ for }k=0\penalty\ ,\\
0\text{ for }k>0\penalty\ .\end{cases}

Expanding for
k>0
,

d_{\rm PV}f(T,T^{n+k})=[T_{\lambda}f(T^{n+k})]-f([T_{\lambda}T^{n+k}])=[T_{\lambda}f(T^{n+k})]-2(n+k)\lambda f(T^{n+k})-\partial f(T^{n+k})\penalty\ .

For
k=1
,
d_{{\textup{PV}}}f(T,T^{n+1})\overset{!}{=}0
implies that

[T_{\lambda}f(T^{n+1})]\overset{!}{=}2(n+1)\lambda f(T^{n+1})+\partial f(T^{n+1})\penalty\ .

So,
f(T^{n+1})\in M_{n}
must have conformal weight
2(n+1)
and its
\lambda
-bracket with
T
has no terms of order
\lambda^{2}
or higher—but this implies that
f(T^{n}+1)
has no derivatives. The only candidate for
f(T^{n+1})
is, therefore, a scalar multiple of
T^{n+1}
, which vanishes in
M_{n}
. Thus, we conclude that
f(T^{n+1})=0
and by (
9.3
),

f(T^{n+k})=0\penalty\ ,\text{ for }k\geq 1\penalty\ .

Moreover,
f(T^{n+1})=Tf(T^{n})=0
and so
f(T^{n})
must be of the form

f(T^{n})=c\,T^{n-2}\partial T+T^{n-1}g

for some
c\in{\mathbb{K}}
and
g\in M_{n}
since these are the only complementary zero divisors of
T
in
M_{n}
. To order
\lambda^{2}
,

d_{\rm PV}f(T,T^{n})=(-c\,T^{n-2}\partial T)\lambda+(\Delta_{g}-1)T^{n-1}g\lambda+O(\lambda^{2})\penalty\ .

The consistency conditions of (
9.5
) state that
d_{\rm PV}f(T,T^{n})
must be in the algebraic ideal generated by
T^{n-1}
, which forces
c=0
. But,
f(T^{n-1})=T^{n-1}g
is actually the restriction of the derivation

F=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{m}\partial^{m}g\frac{\partial}{\partial\partial^{m}T}\in{\textup{Der}}_{\partial}(V,M_{n})\penalty\ ,

to
I_{n}
. Therefore, under the identification of (
9.4
),
[d_{{\textup{AQ}}}f]=0\in H^{1}_{{\textup{AQ}}}(M_{n},M_{n})
and so the kernel of the
E_{1}
-differential is trivial, as desired.
∎

9.5.3.
The variational cohomology

To compute the variational cohomology of
M_{n}
, we make use of the same strategy: once again, we have an inclusion of complexes

C^{\bullet}_{\rm PV}(M_{n},M_{n})\xrightarrow{\iota}C^{\bullet}_{\rm PV}(V,M_{n})\penalty\ ,

with image equal to the subcomplex of cochains in
C^{\bullet}_{\rm PV}(V,M_{n})
vanishing on
I_{n}
. We write
{\textup{coker}}(\iota)
for the cokernel complex of
\iota
.
In degree zero, we have the identification

{\textup{coker}}^{0}(\iota)\cong{\textup{Der}}_{\partial}(V,M_{n})|_{I_{n}}

with the quotient map
C^{0}_{\rm PV}(V,M_{n})\rightarrow{\textup{Der}}_{\partial}(V,M_{n})|_{I_{n}}
given by restriction of vector fields. The cohomology
H^{0}({\textup{coker}}(\iota))
consists of
f\in{\textup{Der}}_{\partial}(V,M_{n})|_{I_{n}}
such that

(9.6)

[a_{\lambda}f(b)]=f([a_{\lambda}b])\penalty\ ,

for
a\in V
and
b\in I_{n}
,
i.e.
, the cocycles intertwine the
{\textup{Lie}}^{*}
-action of
V
.

Lemma 9.12
.

The zeroth cohomology of the cokernel vanishes, i.e.,

H^{0}({\textup{coker}}(\iota))=0

Proof.

Since
{\textup{coker}}^{0}(\iota)\cong{\textup{Der}}(V,M_{n})|_{I_{n}}
, we can represent our putative cocycle as
F|_{I_{n}}
for some derivation
F\in{\textup{Der}}_{\partial}(V,M_{n})
. Then by (
9.6
) this cochain is closed if and only if it intertwines the
{\textup{Lie}}^{*}
-action of
V
, and so

[T_{\lambda}F(T^{n})]\overset{!}{=}F[T_{\lambda}T^{n}]=2n\lambda F(T^{n})+\partial F(T^{n})\penalty\ .

This in turn implies that
F(T^{n})
has conformal weight
2n
and has no derivatives, which implies that
F(T^{n})
is a scalar multiple of
T^{n}
, which vanishes in the quotient. Therefore,
F|_{I_{n}}=0\in{\textup{coker}}^{0}(\iota)
as desired.
∎

Lemma 9.13

(
[
BDSK20
]
)
.

The first variational cohomology
H^{1}_{\rm PV}(V,M_{n})
is one dimensional and spanned by the class of
C\in C^{1}_{\rm var}(V,M_{n})
, with

C_{\lambda}(T,T)=\lambda^{3}\penalty\ .

Proof.

The proof is essentially the same as that of Theorem 4.17 in
[
BDSK20
]
, which computes
H^{1}_{{{\textup{PV}}}}(V,V)={\mathbb{K}}
. The change of coefficients to
M_{n}
does not affect the proof strategy overmuch.
∎

Proposition 9.14
.

The first variational cohomology of
M_{n}
vanishes, i.e.,

H^{1}_{\rm PV}(M_{n},M_{n})=0\penalty\ .

Proof.

The short exact sequence
0\rightarrow C^{\bullet}_{\rm PV}(M_{n},M_{n})\xrightarrow{\iota}C^{\bullet}_{\rm PV}(V,M_{n})\rightarrow{\textup{coker}}^{\bullet}(\iota)\rightarrow 0
induces the usual long exact sequence in cohomology,

(9.7)

\dots\rightarrow H^{0}({\textup{coker}}(\iota))\rightarrow H^{1}_{\rm PV}(M_{n},M_{n})\rightarrow H^{1}_{\rm PV}(V,M_{n})\rightarrow H^{1}({\textup{coker}}(\iota))\rightarrow\dots\penalty\ .

First, we show that
H^{1}_{\rm PV}(V,M_{n})\rightarrow H^{1}({\textup{coker}}(\iota))
is injective. By Lemma
9.13
,
H^{1}_{\rm PV}(V,M_{n})
is spanned by the class
[C]
and since

C_{\lambda}(T,T^{n})=nT^{n-1}\lambda^{3}\neq 0\penalty\ ,

its image in
{\textup{coker}}^{1}(\iota)
is nonzero. Because
C^{\bullet}_{\rm PV}(V,M_{n})\rightarrow{\textup{coker}}^{\bullet}(\iota)
is surjective the image of
[C]
in
H^{1}({\textup{coker}}(\iota))
is nonzero and
H^{1}_{\rm PV}(V,M_{n})\rightarrow H^{1}({\textup{coker}}(\iota))
is injective.

Therefore, by exactness, the map
H^{1}_{\rm PV}(M_{n},M_{n})\rightarrow H^{1}_{\rm PV}(V,M_{n})
is identically zero. And so, again by exactness,

H^{1}_{\rm PV}(M_{n},M_{n})={\textup{im}}\big(H^{0}({\textup{coker}}(\iota))\rightarrow H^{1}_{\rm PV}(M_{n},M_{n})\big)\penalty\ ,

But, by Lemma
9.12
,
H^{0}({\textup{coker}}(\iota))=0
and thus
H^{1}_{\rm PV}(M_{n},M_{n})=0
, as desired.
∎

9.5.4.

Combining the results of the preceding sections, we have:

Proposition 9.15
.

For any
n\in\mathbb{N}
,

H^{1}_{\rm c}(M_{n},M_{n})=0\penalty\ ,

and, therefore

H^{1}_{\rm ch}({\mathcal{M}}_{n},{\mathcal{M}}_{n})=0\penalty\ .

Proof.

Recall from (
9.2
), that
H^{1}_{c}(M_{n},M_{n})=E_{3}^{1,0}\oplus E_{3}^{0,1}
for the spectral sequence arising from the bicomplex structure of Proposition
5.39
. But by Lemma
9.11

E_{2}^{0,1}=0
and so
E_{3}^{0,1}=0
.

Furthermore,
E_{2}^{1,0}=H_{\rm PV}^{1}(M_{n},M_{n})
, which vanishes by Proposition
9.14
. Therefore,
E_{3}^{1,0}=0
and we conclude that
H^{1}_{c}(M_{n},M_{n})=0
.

Note that the vertex algebra
{\mathcal{M}}_{n}
has a split filtration induced by assigning the generator,
T
, with weight one under
\mathbb{G}_{\hbar}
. Moreover, the associated graded of
{\mathcal{M}}_{n}
, with respect to this filtration, can be identified with
M_{n}
, as vertex Poisson algebras. This is the content of Lemma
4.30
.

Therefore, by Proposition
7.20
, the dimension of
H^{1}_{\rm ch}({\mathcal{M}}_{n},{\mathcal{M}}_{n})
is bounded by the dimension of
H^{1}_{c}(M_{n},M_{n})
, giving the desired result.

∎

This gives us two immediate corollaries.

Theorem 9.16
.

The Virasoro minimal model
{\mathcal{M}}_{\hbar,n}
is the unique (up to isomorphism), graded quantization of the vertex Poisson algebra
M_{n}
.

Proof.

Follows from Corollary
7.31
noting that the classical cohomology vanishes by Proposition
9.15
.
∎

Theorem 9.17
.

The Virasoro minimal model
{\mathcal{M}}_{n}
is rigid and admits no deformations.

Proof.

Follows from the vanishing of
H^{1}_{\rm ch}({\mathcal{M}}_{n},{\mathcal{M}}_{n})
in Proposition
9.15
.
∎
